#3028. Google Wallet comes to your iPhone – but without this feature

Tech > Services & Software > Operating Systems > Mobile OS > iOS

The new Google Wallet app brings Gemini agentic shopping to US-based iOS users - sorry, no tap-to-pay.

The Google Wallet app is available for iPhone users in the US for the first time. iOS users who download the new Google Wallet app can manage online payments for faster checkout at places that support Google Pay. However, Apple restricts Google’s ability to offer tap-to-pay service on the iOS app, so users won’t be able to use Google Wallet for contactless checkout on iPhone.

Also: You can use Google’s Spark AI agent, renamed Tasks, for free now – with limits

The new Google Wallet app lets you digitize physical cards, including payment, loyalty, and membership cards, as well as boarding passes. Google Wallet app users on iOS will also be able to view receipts and track orders pulled from Gmail in one app.

There used to be a Google Pay app for iOS, but it was discontinued in 2024. The old Google Pay app also didn’t support tap-to-pay on iPhone, but it offered limited functionality, including support for loyalty and gift cards, as well as purchase tracking.

The biggest difference between the old Google Pay app for iOS and the new Google Wallet is the AI component, which supports agentic shopping, likely as Google’s longer-term play.

One of Google’s goals for the Wallet app on iOS is to make it a central place to manage purchases made by its AI agents. The Agents section in Google Wallet for iPhone lets you manage and monitor purchases made through Gemini.

Agentic commerce is on the rise, as evidenced by the growing use of Meta’s Muse. Gemini can make purchases and payments through the Tasks feature, which lets you give Gemini permission to buy the items you want and set your maximum budget.

Also: Google Wallet just turned your Android phone into a digital house key

The Google Wallet app for iOS becomes the central hub for purchase management in agentic commerce through Gemini. In it, you can view active tasks to see which purchases Gemini is working on, as well as completed purchases and receipts.

Google has been working on two technologies to support its agentic commerce strategy: Universal Commerce Protocol (UCP) and Agent Payments Protocol (AP2).

UCP is a technical language that enables AI agents to communicate with retailers and make purchases, while AP2 is the payment framework that establishes rules and authorization for agents to make payments.

Google Wallet serves as both the AI-powered wallet and the consumer-facing record of transactions made by Gemini.

Also: Google Wallet has a lot of new tricks – these 5 are my favorites

Along with Google, major retailers supported the development of UCP, including Shopify, Walmart, Target, and Etsy. When Google announced AP2, more than 60 organizations became involved, including PayPal, Salesforce, Mastercard, and American Express.

#3027. Yes, You Can Invest in Grand Theft Auto VI (GTA 6). Here's How.

Investors have several options for buying GTA 6-related investments.

By Will Healy – Nov 18, 2026 at 7:00PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Take-Two Interactive Software ›

The release of the Grand Theft Auto VI (GTA 6) video game is finally upon us. No new version of this extremely popular interactive digital entertainment has come out since the release of GTA 5 in 2013, and since the release of the first iteration of the game in 1997, it has ranked as one of the best-selling gaming franchises of all time.

Not surprisingly, the anticipation has drawn the interest of gamers and investors alike. Fortunately, the GTA 6 release presents an opportunity for shareholders; here's how investors can capitalize.

The most straightforward way to invest in this game is to buy Take-Two Interactive (TTWO +6.22%) stock. Take-Two is the parent company of Rockstar Games, the studio that developed the game. The game's launch brings to mind multiple takeaways for long-term investors, raised just after the company released its latest earnings report. Take-Two stock rose by 6.6% in the trading session that closed hours before the game's release. However, the stock has dropped over the last year largely due to the company's past delays in releasing the game.

In the first half of fiscal 2027 (ended Sept. 30), revenue of $3 billion fell by 8% from year-ago levels. Analysts blamed weaker in-game spending on NBA 2K and the anticipation of GTA 6 as reasons for the decline.

Also, the company reported an $88 million loss during the same period, though analysts forecast a return to profitability in upcoming quarters. For now, Take-Two's price-to-sales (P/S) ratio is 6.3. That is slightly above the five-year average of 5.3 and suggests investors will pay a modest premium by buying the stock now.

Amid that high valuation, some investors might not be comfortable owning Take-Two stock. Others might see the GTA 6 release as an opportunity for gaming in general. Such investors may choose to invest through an exchange-traded fund (ETF), with multiple opportunities available.

The ETF with the highest exposure to Take-Two is the VanEck Digital Native Economy ETF (GENZ +0.77%). Approximately 8.25% of the fund's holdings are in Take-Two. Unfortunately, this comes with a 0.51% expense ratio, just above the industry average of 0.48%. Moreover, it lost 26% of its value over the last five years, further dampening its appeal.

The one with the best five-year performance is the VanEck Video Gaming and eSports ETF (ESPO +1.55%), which returned about 35% over that time. Its exposure to Take-Two is slightly less, just 6.05%. Still, the higher return probably makes the 0.55% expense ratio more palatable.

Another fund to consider is the State Street Communication Services Select SPDR ETF (XLC +0.30%). It returned 31% over the last five years, and its 0.08% expense ratio makes it cheap to own. Still, Take-Two makes up just 4.75% of the fund's holdings, and most of the stocks are not in gaming.

As one of the best-selling gaming franchises of all time, the GTA 6 release looks to be huge, and investors have many options to capitalize on the opportunity.

Investing in the launch of GTA 6 involves holding a position in Rockstar Games' parent company, Take-Two, either directly or through an ETF. Whatever direction investors take, the revival of one of gaming's most popular franchises is an opportunity that does not come along often.

Knowing that, investors probably have good reason to at least consider investments tied to the communication stock and, possibly, the gaming industry in general.

#3026. Critical Java Flaw Leaves Systems Open to Hackers

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Tech > Services & Software > Cybersecurity

The bug could allow hackers to take over systems without a password.

A newly identified vulnerability in Oracle Java could give hackers unauthorized access to major applications and allow them to run code without logging in. Major tech companies like Google, Amazon, Microsoft, Apple and Netflix, which rely heavily on Java’s server-side software and core products, could be affected.

The flaw, tracked as CVE-2026-114938 (and dubbed Java4Shell), corrupts memory when Java decodes Base64, a standard format used to transmit login credentials and file uploads.

Thai Duong, the CEO of cybersecurity firm Calif, tells CNET that his company identified the critical software vulnerability in October and notified Oracle, which has since patched the vulnerability. Now, the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency is urging organizations to do the same.

Tech giants like Google, Amazon, Microsoft, Apple and Netflix rely heavily on Java.

“Roughly 90% of Fortune 500 companies run on Java,” Duong said.

Oracle rated the flaw a serious 8.1 out of 10 on the standard vulnerability scale. It can lead to an unauthenticated remote code execution vulnerability in Java, allowing hackers to run their own malicious commands or code over the internet without requiring a username, password or prior access.

Federico Kirschbaum, head of Security Lab, the research arm at cybersecurity company XBOW, tells CNET that Java is primarily used at the enterprise level. As a result, the vulnerability could trickle across different sectors, including finance, government, transportation and large corporations.

“Java is a great language for creating solutions, but this vulnerability — it’s a reminder that maybe the languages that we’re using have flaws, and everything built on those might inherit that problem,” Kirschbaum said.

Amid recent technological disruptions, he suggests leaning on AI solutions to address vulnerabilities in AI.

“That new normal is that most of the technology we rely on has flaws, and now that we have resources using LLMs, we can find those problems,” Kirschbaum said.

He points out that bad actors and hackers can also find these vulnerabilities.

“We are in a race to find the problems first and help the industry by guiding them on what’s urgent so they patch it,” Kirschbaum said.

Since Calif discovered Java4Shell in October, the US Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency has issued guidance urging organizations to patch it immediately.

The agency recommends identifying affected Java installations, reviewing software inventory for JDK’s and Java runtimes and applying relevant vendor patches as soon as they’re available.

#3025. After Nvidia’s Earnings, Here’s My Prediction for the Stock Price by the End of the Year

Nvidia stock has dropped more than 30% in just a few weeks.

By Adria Cimino – Nov 18, 2026 at 7:05PM EST

Nvidia (NVDA -2.90%) has seen its stock price slide in recent days -- and this hasn't been linked to the company's earnings performance or market position. The artificial intelligence (AI) chip leader fell following allegations that chief executive officer Jensen Huang had at least two affairs. Huang stepped down, and the board named finance chief Colette Kress as interim leader.

Kress conducted her first earnings call as interim CEO this week and presented an Nvidia that investors know well: a company serving soaring demand and delivering earnings that once again beat analysts' expectations. The entire report was very positive and suggested many more bright days ahead for the AI giant.

Still, it's important to keep in mind that some uncertainty remains following Huang's resignation.

After Nvidia's earnings report and amid the current turbulence, here's my prediction for the stock price by the end of the year.

First, though, let's take a look at the complete Nvidia picture, including its successes in recent years. Nvidia has built an AI empire, starting with graphics processing units (GPUs) and broadening its offerings to include networking tools, enterprise software, and most recently stand-alone central processing units (CPUs). GPUs generally power AI workloads, but CPUs now have emerged as a chip that's useful in agentic AI -- so CPUs may represent a key growth engine in the years to come.

Nvidia's efforts have kept it in the lead globally and resulted in mind-boggling earnings growth. This has continued into the recent quarter, with revenue and net income soaring in the triple digits and demand marching higher too. In this fiscal third quarter, Nvidia reported a 103% increase in revenue to $115 billion, and net income came in at about $65 billion. Kress raised the forecast for revenue growth in the next fiscal year to 75%, up from the earlier guidance of 70%.

Importantly, the company just rolled out its latest platform update, Vera Rubin, and it's "off to an extraordinary start," Kress said. Rubin brought in $20 billion in revenue in its first quarter.

Meanwhile, Nvidia isn't the only AI company to speak of ongoing high demand; fellow chip designers and cloud service providers have delivered the same message. At the same time, due to the recent turmoil, Nvidia's stock has fallen to a bargain valuation, trading at 16x forward earnings estimates. All of these points may encourage investors to buy the stock.

Still, Huang's departure might continue to weigh on Nvidia to some extent. Nvidia's board is investigating allegations that Huang had a relationship with a woman U.S. intelligence calls a Chinese spy -- and Congress has called on Huang to testify in early December in relation to the matter. This could result in government scrutiny of Nvidia and potentially even further regulation of chip sales to China. Such uncertainty, along with the fact that we don't yet know who will serve as permanent CEO, could weigh on the stock.

It's impossible to predict stock performance with great accuracy, of course, but based on these elements I've presented here, my prediction is Nvidia's stock will regain a bit of territory that it lost in recent times. It's declined more than 30% in just a few weeks.

The encouraging earnings report, strength in demand that is ongoing, and the stability provided by Kress are all very positive points. And the company's current valuation is extremely low, particularly considering the idea that its technology remains unchanged and customer relationships are strong. This should keep innovation going and sales marching higher.

Nvidia's stock performance may see some ups and downs in these final weeks of the year as investors weigh the positive points I mentioned above and the uncertainties. Considering this, I predict that Nvidia stock may finish the year with a gain of about 10% from the Nov. 17 closing price of $155 -- and that would put the stock at around $170 by the end of 2026.

One thing to keep in mind, however, is that you shouldn't buy or sell Nvidia stock to capture a potential gain or avoid a possible loss over such a short period of time; instead, it's important to focus on the company's prospects over the long term before making an investment move.

#3024. Survey: Gen Z Loves a Winter Challenge, but Money Goals Often Fizzle

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Gen Zers are apt to try cold-weather challenges like “winter arc.” But they’re also the generation most likely to say they generally quit challenges before finishing and set the same money goal more than once due to not sticking with it.

It’s not technically winter yet, but depending on where you live, it may feel like it. For many of us, this is a time of hibernation, but for others, it’s prime time for a glow-up.

“Winter arc” is a popular social media challenge through which people aim to improve themselves before the new year. It typically runs from October through December, but can be started any time during the cold months. The aim is to jump-start the “new year, new me” season before the ball even drops.

Whether they call it a winter arc or not, plenty of Americans are working on self-improvement as the chill sets in. According to a new NerdWallet survey, conducted online by The Harris Poll, 75% of Americans say they’re working on their physical health, and 69% say the same about their finances.

Popular as it is, winter arc isn’t the only cold-weather challenge out there. The survey found that half of Americans (50%) say they’ve tried some sort of winter improvement challenge. And no one likes a challenge like Gen Z: Nearly three-quarters of Gen Zers (74%) have tried a winter challenge, compared with 64% of millennials, 42% of Gen Xers and 29% of baby boomers.

Note: The survey defines Gen Zers as those 18-29; millennials, ages 30-45; Gen Xers, ages 46-61 and baby boomers, ages 62-80.

But there’s a difference between starting a challenge and successfully completing one. Gen Zers reign supreme as those most likely to hop on a winter challenge. But they’re also the most likely to say they generally quit challenges before finishing, buy products they saw on social media in an effort to stick with the challenges and set the same financial goal more than once.

Gen Zers are more likely than any other generation to say they:

Have tried a winter financial challenge other than no-spend January (21%, vs. 11% of millennials, 5% of Gen Xers and 2% of baby boomers).

Generally quit challenges before they finish them (59%, vs. 42% of millennials, 36% of Gen Xers and 22% of baby boomers).

Expect to spend more on at least one discretionary expense this winter than they would during another season (90%, vs. 83% of millennials, 61% of Gen Xers and 48% of baby boomers).

Put off dealing with money until after the holidays (60%, vs. 47% of millennials, 35% of Gen Xers and 15% of baby boomers).

Aren’t currently working on improving their finances, but want to make it a priority (15%, vs. 8% of millennials and 6% each of Gen Xers and baby boomers).

“Embracing the winter arc trend can be a great way to find motivation to make progress on a goal over the slower winter months,” says Kimberly Palmer, personal finance expert at NerdWallet. “Pick a winter challenge that aligns with a longer-term goal for the coming year.”

Winter challenges popular with Gen Z include diet- or fitness-related challenges (29%) and self-improvement challenges, like winter arc or 75 Hard, a mental toughness challenge that runs for 75 days and includes two workouts every day, a strict diet and daily nonfiction reading (25%).

While winter arcs typically focus on physical, mental and intellectual self-improvement, Gen Z is game to try a financial challenge as well: One long-standing financial challenge — no-spend January — is more popular among younger Americans (22% of Gen Zers and 20% of millennials) than their older counterparts (12% of Gen Xers and 6% of baby boomers). As for other types of financial challenges, Gen Z is more likely than any other generation to have tried at least one: 21% compared with 11% of millennials, 5% of Gen X and 2% of baby boomers.

Of course, it’s one thing to try a challenge, during wintertime or otherwise. It’s another thing entirely to follow through and complete it. That’s the hard part for many Gen Zers.

When taking on a self-improvement challenge, the beginning tends to be easiest. You’re motivated and excited to reach goals and better yourself. But as time goes on, it’s easy to slip back into old habits or for the shine of a brand-new challenge to dim. And Gen Zers report that they are most likely to quit early.

According to the survey, nearly 3 in 5 Gen Zers (59%) say they generally quit challenges before they finish them. That compares with 42% of millennials, 36% of Gen Xers and 22% of baby boomers.

Along with challenges in general, money goals can be difficult to follow through with, especially for younger Americans. Around two-thirds of Gen Zers (64%) say it’s been hard to stick to financial goals they’ve set in the past, compared with 52% of Gen Xers and 34% of baby boomers. Gen Zers are more likely than any other generation to say they’ve set the same financial goal more than once because they didn’t stick with it — 33%, compared with 20% of millennials, 15% of Gen Xers and 7% of baby boomers.

This isn’t for lack of desire, though. Seven in 10 Gen Zers (70%) say they’re working on improving their finances. All generations show high levels of effort here: 76% each of millennials and Gen Xers say the same, along with 59% of baby boomers.

So why is it so hard to stick with money goals? One possibility is that it’s difficult to see meaningful progress on finances, particularly when you’re young. According to the survey, 65% of Gen Zers say it’s harder to see progress on financial goals than on physical goals.

This makes perfect sense for this age group — for those light on money but rich in youthfulness, desired physical changes may show up faster than financial progress. On top of that, the current job market is tough for younger Americans. Recent college grads now have a higher unemployment rate than workers in general. Even if you’re still on the job hunt, your 20s are a great time to start taking steps, however small, toward building future wealth.» MORE: I’m 25. Here’s How I Got Started Investing

“To increase your chances of success, try coordinating winter arc plans with a friend or giving yourself smaller goals along the way,” Palmer says. “Leaning on the support or coaching from a loved one can make it easier to keep going, as can celebrating incremental progress.”

Self-improvement is a noble pursuit. But as with most things in life, it’s easy to lose sight of the ultimate goal in favor of buying things to help you reach it.

“It’s easy to feel like a quick purchase can help us meet our goals, and sometimes a product can be a useful tool, but the reality is that true winter arc progress comes from putting in the hard work. There’s no option for ‘purchasing’ it instead,” Palmer says.

According to the survey, more than a quarter of Gen Zers (26%) say they’ve bought a product they saw on social media to help with a personal goal or challenge. This is more than any other generation: 17% of millennials, 11% of Gen Xers and 7% of baby boomers say they’ve made social media buys for self-betterment.

Some self-improvement purchases may be billed on a monthly basis. More than 1 in 5 Gen Zers (23%) say they're still paying for a subscription or membership they signed up for during a past goal or challenge. This is also true for 17% of millennials, 9% of Gen Xers and 2% of baby boomers.

These challenge purchases may pile onto already higher winter expenses. A majority of Gen Zers (90%) say they expect to spend more on at least one discretionary expense this winter than they would during another season, more than any other generation. Top expenses include food delivery (38%), entertainment at home (35%) and indoor family entertainment (30%). A quarter of Gen Zers (25%) expect to spend more on fitness or wellness apps this winter compared to other seasons.

Some may be spending more to make themselves feel better during the cold, gray months. Nearly 1 in 5 Gen Zers (18%) say they tend to combat winter blues by spending money, compared with 10% of Gen Xers and 3% of baby boomers.

The holiday season is an expensive time of year for many of us, and it’s easy to sweep finances under the rug until the new year. According to the survey, 3 in 5 Gen Zers (60%) say they generally put off dealing with money until after the holidays. This is more than any other generation: 47% of millennials, 35% of Gen Xers and 15% of baby boomers say the same.

Even for those taking on financial challenges before the new year hits, it’s easy for the resulting “savings” to disappear. Nearly three-quarters of Gen Zers (74%) say money they save by cutting back in one area usually just gets spent somewhere else instead of staying “saved.” This is also true for 68% of millennials, 63% of Gen Xers and 45% of baby boomers.

For some, the holidays are just one thing competing for their money. Over half of Gen Zers (52%) say they’d rather spend money on how they look now than save it for their future financial security. This compares with 26% of Gen Xers and 13% of baby boomers.

We don’t begrudge anyone for their desire to look and feel their best this upcoming season. But that doesn’t have to mean you can’t level up your money at the same time.

More than 2 in 5 Gen Zers (43%) say they’re putting consistent work into improving their finances, and 27% are putting some work into this, but not consistently. Moreover, 15% of Gen Zers aren’t working on improving their finances but want to make them a priority. No matter your age, here are some ideas for a financial glow-up before the spring thaw.

Start small if you tend to quit. Nearly 2 in 5 Americans (38%) say they generally quit challenges before finishing them. Early-challenge motivation can make it easy to overcommit. If you often take on challenges impulsively then peter out a few weeks later, try starting small. For example, maybe no-spend January is a stretch, but you can still save by intentionally creating two no-spend days a week.

Move money the day you save it. Over 3 in 5 Americans (61%) say money they save by cutting back in one area usually gets spent somewhere else. If you’re cutting back, transfer the difference to savings that same day, so it can’t leak out elsewhere.

Make money progress measurable. Half of Americans (50%) say it’s harder to see progress on financial goals than physical goals. There’s a saying that what gets measured, gets managed. Choose one number to track. This might be down payment savings, debt payoff progress or an investment milestone. It may not move much month to month, but you’d be surprised at the difference a year can make, even without a ton of money each month to dedicate toward your goal.

Begin before January. More than a third of Americans (37%) say they put off dealing with money until after the holidays. Facing your finances during the often busy and expensive final months of the year can be daunting, but don’t put your head in the sand until after the holiday credit card balances come due. Put a cap on your winter spending before the new year to avoid going overboard during the tempting sales this time of year.

“If you’re trying to choose a winter arc, pick one that’s realistic but also a stretch goal, so it’s satisfying to accomplish while not being completely out of reach,” Palmer suggests. “Chances are you’ll be glad with what you got done over the colder months when springtime hits.”

#3023. Voyager 1 Becomes the First Spacecraft to Travel a Full Light-Day From Earth

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Voyager 1 has been flying at about 38,000 mph since 1980. That steady pace carried it a full light-day from home.

NASA’s Voyager 1 left Earth on Sept. 5, 1977, carrying a camera, a set of science instruments and a gold-plated record of sounds and images from home. Forty-nine years later, it’s reached a distance no spacecraft has attained before: one light-day, or roughly 16.1 billion miles, from Earth. At that range, light needs a full 24 hours to make the trip between Earth and the probe.

At 5:16:07 a.m. ET on Wednesday, Voyager 1 became the first human-made object to reach that light-day milestone. From that point on, a radio signal between the spacecraft and NASA’s Deep Space Network takes more than a day to travel in either direction.

NASA puts the journey at 49 years, two months and 11 days, counting from Voyager 1’s launch in September 1977. The probe still sends data home, though at a rate slower than dial-up, and NASA plans a live discussion with the Voyager team on Thursday to mark the occasion.

It’s quite an accomplishment for a spacecraft built in the mid-1970s. One light-day is the distance that light travels in a single day at the speed of light, which is approximately 186,282 miles per second. That translates to 25.9 billion kilometers or about 16.1 billion miles in a single day. At an average human walking speed of 3 mph, it would take a human 612,015 years to travel that distance on foot.

Fortunately, Voyager 1 has been traveling a bit faster. It currently zips through the cosmos at a steady 37,763 mph, a speed it achieved in 1980 after a gravity-assisted Saturn flyby. It has been flying at that speed ever since.

The milestone comes 14 years after Voyager 1 crossed the heliopause, the point where the sun’s heliosphere ends, and entered interstellar space. It’s also 28 years after the spacecraft passed Pioneer 10 to become the human-made object that has traveled the farthest into space.

NASA marked the milestone with a global Shine Bright for Voyager’s Light-Day event on Tuesday evening. Just after sunset, the agency wanted people to head outside and point their smartphone or tablet lights skyward to spark a planet-wide wave of light as Earth rotates, much like fans doing the wave at a stadium.

The space agency also made a website for the occasion. The website is simple. It flashes Voyager’s VGR call sign in Morse code. If you want to try it, click the link, hit begin, and then point your phone or tablet screen at the sky. The website does all the work for you.

NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory will follow up on Thursday at noon ET with a moderated discussion on the milestone, featuring status updates on both Voyager probes and a live Q&A with reporters and audience members.

The historic milestone isn’t without its drama. Voyager 1 has been in space for nearly half a century, and in that time, the Jet Propulsion Laboratory has done everything it can to extend the spacecraft’s life. That includes troubleshooting issues, performing maintenance, and shutting down various systems to save power and extend the spacecraft’s lifespan.

Several of those efforts took place in 2026. The team running Voyager 1 spent weeks shutting down three devices and powering on three low-power alternatives to reduce overall power usage. This initiative, which was internally called the Big Bang, was also performed on Voyager 2. The transition was completed in early September, giving Voyager 1 more time with its dwindling power supply while also keeping it warm enough to continue operating in the frigid conditions of interstellar space.

It was a tricky maneuver that required the team to simultaneously turn multiple instruments on and off. The difficulty of the maneuver was amplified by the fact that it took almost a full day for a signal to reach Voyager and another full day for Voyager to send a signal back, with hours of downtime while the spacecraft performed the operations.

All of this took place several months after Voyager 1 experienced a sudden drop in power, which necessitated shutting off one of its three instruments still in operation. Voyager 1 now has only two operational instruments.

Making it a whole light-day away from Earth is likely going to be Voyager 1’s final milestone while it still has enough power to communicate with Earth. Voyager 1 is powered by three radioisotope thermoelectric generators. They are essentially nuclear-powered batteries that generate energy by converting the steady heat from decaying plutonium-238.

When Voyager 1 first launched, its radioisotope thermoelectric generators produced about 470 watts of power. But plutonium-238 doesn’t last forever, and after 50 years in space, those generators now produce about 215 watts of power, with an average loss of about 4 watts per year. Most of that power is used to keep Voyager 1 up and running, which is why NASA has shut down eight of the 10 instruments that Voyager had when it launched.

At its current rate of usage and decay, Voyager 1 is expected to shut down its final science instrument in the 2030s and will eventually lose communication with Earth in 2036 due to insufficient power to send back a radio signal.

The same fate is expected for Voyager 2, Voyager 1’s twin, which has also spent half a century in space. While it is labeled the sequel, Voyager 2 actually launched 16 days before Voyager 1, and the two have spent nearly identical amounts of time traversing the cosmos. Thanks to its slower speed and trajectory, it likely won’t reach the one light-day distance until after its instruments shut down.

Voyager 1 will lose all of its power before it hits its next milestone, which is the beginning of the Oort Cloud, a vast, icy shell of comets at the edge of our solar system. It’ll take about 300 years for Voyager 1 to make it there.

Thanks to the near-perfect vacuum of outer space, Voyager 1 will continue to fly at its current speed even after it shuts down entirely. So it’ll still hit that milestone; it just won’t be able to phone home to tell us about it.

#3022. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

The stock market continues to grapple with volatility amid geopolitical tensions in the Middle East and concerns about the sustainability of the AI boom. Given this uncertainty, investors looking for steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick the right dividend stocks, as these experts assign their ratings after an in-depth analysis of a company's financials and ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Downstream energy company Phillips 66 (PSX) is this week's first dividend pick. With a quarterly dividend $1.27 per share (annualized dividend of $5.08), PSX offers a yield of 2.25%. The company recently reported solid Q2 earnings, as the Middle East conflict affected global supplies and drove up refining margins.

Following the Q2 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Phillips 66 and raised his price target to $255 from $240, reflecting higher 2026 earnings expectations and lower interest expense next year.

Gabelman highlighted the quarter-over-quarter reduction in Phillips 66's net debt, noting management's optimism at achieving its estimated $15.5 billion net debt target one year ahead of schedule. The 5-star analyst expects the company to end 2026 with a net debt of $14.6 billion.

"The [balance sheet] improvement could re-establish PSX as a go-to defensive refiner," said Gabelman.

Management agreed that PSX's payout ratio lagged year-to-date, but expects buybacks to increase in the second half. Gabelman said Phillps indicated the possibility of a larger dividend hike following an annual increase of 5% over the past two years.

Gabelman ranks No. 554 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Phillips 66 Statistics on TipRanks.

Crescent Energy (CRGY) is an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian and Uinta Basins. Earlier this month, the company reported better-than-expected Q2 earnings and announced a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share, payable on August 31. At an annualized dividend of $0.48 per share, CRGY boasts a dividend yield of about 4%.

In reaction to Q2 results, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reaffirmed a buy rating on Crescent Energy stock with a price target of $18. He noted continued strength in the company's performance, with second-quarter oil production and cash flow surpassing the Street's expectations.

"CRGY's cash flow exceeded expectations by 10%, reinforcing its trajectory of capital efficiency," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that Crescent raised its full-year oil production guidance, citing the effective integration of the Vital Energy acquisition. Specifically, Crescent tripled its synergy target from the Vital Energy deal to as much as $300 million, significantly reducing the effective purchase price. This reflects Crescent's solid execution following the acquisition, Richardson said.

Additionally, the analyst noted that Crescent's capital spending is and heading toward the lower end of management's prior guidance, indicating disciplined financial controls.

Richardson ranks No. 579 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 65% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.5%. See Crescent Energy Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Finally, Viper Energy (VNOM) is effectively controlled by Diamondback Energy. Viper owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests in oil-weighted basins, mainly the Permian in West Texas. Viper recently announced its Q2 2026 results and declared a 32% hike in its base dividend, effective in the third quarter of 2026. The new base dividend indicates a 4.5% annualized yield.

Viper also removed its quarterly commitment to return at least 75% of cash available for distribution. The company believes that this move will ensure increased flexibility to make opportunistic share repurchases and pursue accretive mergers and acquisitions.

Following the Q2 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper, slightly raising his price target to $59 from $58. Bilkoski said Viper's solid Q2 performance supports his view that the strength in oil prices and rising operator activity are driving higher production.

"Viper has delivered, and we forecast will continue to deliver, one of the highest production per share growth profiles in our royalty universe" through the end of 2027, said Bilkoski. He believes Viper deserves a premium valuation, given its above-average growth.

The 5-star analyst said that while the biggest surprise in the quarter was Viper's revision to its shareholder return framework, he doesn't believe that the move indicates a significant change to its underlying strategy. Under the new framework, a larger portion of excess free cash flow is expected to be allocated to share repurchases than to variable dividends, Bilkoski said.

Bilkoski ranks No. 719 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Viper Energy Financials on TipRanks.

Grid bottlenecks are driving customer demand, and management's expanding generator rentals while targeting positive Adjusted EBITDA (adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, an operational profit metric) by late 2027.

Management is prioritizing fleet capital allocation to drive long-term margin growth.

#3019. Mortgage Rates Today, Tuesday, November 17: A Little Higher

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates continue to climb under pressure from inflation.

Mortgage rates keep rising as markets anticipate a near-certain Fed rate hike next month.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage rose to 7.34% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is seven basis points higher than yesterday and 34 basis points higher than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

On Friday, the Bureau of Labor Statistics released the Producer Price Index for October, which tracks the change in prices that U.S. producers are able to get for their products. This is one way to measure inflation on the market end, rather than the consumer end.

The results were mixed. While prices didn’t rise quite as high as economists anticipated month-over-month (0.3% vs. 0.4%), year-over-year price growth did reach 6.5% — the largest increase in four years. This points to even higher costs for consumers coming in the near future.

Investors are convinced that the Federal Reserve will raise overnight borrowing rates again in December to pull inflation down. And higher overnight borrowing rates — called the federal funds rate, which is what banks pay to fund products like mortgages — usually lead to higher mortgage rates.

» MORE: How the Federal Reserve affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.84% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

🤓 Nerdy Reminder: Rates can change daily, and even hourly. If you’re happy with the deal you have, it’s okay to commit.

#3018. Here's What a $1,000 Investment in Kraken Robotics Will Be Worth by the End of 2027 if Undersea Drone Orders Keep Doubling

Down 70% from its high, would a doubling of drone revenue in 2027 be enough to drive a big rebound for Kraken Robotics stock?

By Keith Noonan – Nov 18, 2026 at 7:15PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Kraken Robotics ›

Following strong gains in 2025 and an impressive run-up through the first three months of this year, the tide has turned for Kraken Robotics (KRKNF -3.67%) stock. The company's share price has been hit with strong bearish pressure, and it now trades down 40% over the last year and 70% from the lifetime high that it reached in March.

The company specializes in subsea and maritime technologies, and the batteries and sensor systems it sells for underwater drones are central to its stock story -- but the market appears to have become far less optimistic about how much these products can drive growth. With that in mind, what would it mean for $1,000 invested in Kraken stock at today's prices if undersea drone orders double in 2027?

Before diving more specifically into what a doubling of Kraken's underwater drone component sales in 2027 would mean for the company's stock, it's worth detailing some of the factors that have caused the company's share price to pull back so sharply.

There are several catalysts at play here, and understanding the dynamics will help model the business's growth opportunities in the category in relation to moves for its stock.

In addition to these catalysts, Kraken continues to rely heavily on stock-based compensation to fund its operations. This allows the company to operate without burning through cash, but the creation of new stock dilutes shareholders' positions.

Kraken's subsea drone business focuses on selling batteries and sensors to companies rather than building completed drones itself. In addition to this business, the company also sells towed synthetic aperture sonar (SAS) systems, other subsea sensors, and software. Its business is also significantly more diversified following the acquisition of Covelya. Still, the subsea drone market appears to be Kraken's most important near-term growth driver.

So, what would a doubling of the company's subsea drone revenue in 2027 mean for its valuation? It might not be enough to spur gains in the stock because many investors are banking on even higher growth.

While Kraken is attracting interest from other players in the drone space, Anduril is currently its most important customer by a wide margin. Anduril is the next-gen defense tech company responsible for the Dive-LD and Dive-XL subsea drones and the Ghost Shark drones built with Australia on the Dive-XL platform.

Anduril is working to ramp up production of these drones in 2027, and that could cause Kraken's drone business to more than double if it retains its component wins. For example, Anduril expects that production of Dive-LD drones will increase from roughly 50 this year to its full production rate of roughly 200 by the end of next year. Production for the Dive-XL drones, which are larger and use more batteries, should also see big scaling next year.

So what would a doubling of the company's drone market mean for a $1,000 investment in Kraken stock? Assuming other important dynamics that affect its share price remain relatively constant, I think a doubling of drone-related revenue could mean the stock trades in roughly the same range as now. There may be moderate upside, depending on how the market reacts to the lifting of uncertainty. Big sell-offs this year already seem to bake in softer growth, and a doubling of drone revenue next year would seemingly reflect a ramp that's slower than previously anticipated.

#3017. Missed the Anthropic IPO Allocation? Here’s How to Buy ANT on Thursday.

Anthropic stock will be a hot commodity on Day 1 of its public listing.

By Keithen Drury – Nov 18, 2026 at 7:21PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic, PBC ›

With Anthropic (ANT +0.00%) set to roll out its initial public offering (IPO) on Thursday, not everyone will be able to get in on the expected initial list price of $83. To get that, you had to be a large entity or have access to a brokerage that received some allocation of shares that were priced at the initial price. When a stock goes public, it rarely starts trading at its initial list price due to high demand.

So, if you missed out on the allocation, you'll have to purchase shares on the open market, likely at a higher price than the initial list price.

Will this be worth doing? Let's take a look.

Some stocks execute their IPO without a lot of fanfare. Others get plenty of attention and tend to see their stock skyrocket by the end of the first day of trading. The majority of IPOs follow a common trend: They rise during the first week, then sell off.

Something similar happened with Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +1.27%) earlier this year in what was then the largest IPO in history based on funds raised (Anthropic is projected to surpass it). When measured by valuation, SpaceX is likely to retain the “largest” title. Anthropic was projected to go public at a $2 trillion valuation earlier this year and set a new record, but that won't happen now due to lower interest in AI investments compared to earlier this year.

The lower valuation forecast could end up being a reason why Anthropic doesn't follow the typical initial rise-and-fall pattern, as interest in AI investing has already taken a bite out of Anthropic's initial estimated price tag.

If you want to invest in Anthropic on Day 1, you’ll have to buy shares on the open market, which could mean paying an inflated price compared to what those who got in at the IPO price paid. Still, if Anthropic continues its tremendous growth pace (its revenue increased 12 times year over year to $17 billion in Q3), that price tag could be worth it in a few years.

If you do buy Anthropic shares, just remember that you should be buying for the long term, not to take advantage of a one- or two-day gain. You should be buying the stock to own the company, not to rent it short-term. Anthropic has real potential to be a fantastic business that is rapidly growing and pioneering the AI-first world. It also has risks associated with it that need to be factored in. It has a huge runway, and with the world just scratching the surface of what's possible with AI, now could be a great opportunity to own a winner.

Just because you missed out on the initial allocation of Anthropic stock doesn't mean you have to miss out completely on the IPO.

#3016. Ray Dalio says Bessent move is sign that a debt crisis is getting closer; recommends gold and bitcoin

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent's debt buyback announcement this week fits into a broader pattern that can signal a forthcoming crisis, according to billionaire investor Ray Dalio.

The Bridgewater Associates founder said Bessent's plan to increase government debt purchases may portend trouble for the U.S. economy. Coupled with the Japanese government reducing its U.S. bond market exposure and surging long-dated American bond yields, Dalio said investors may want to prepare their portfolios for increased risk by owning cryptocurrencies and gold.

"I am confident that the government's financial condition is at an inflection point," Dalio wrote in a LinkedIn post published Friday. "If this is not dealt with now, the debts will build up to levels where they can't be managed without great trauma."

Dalio noted that the Treasury Department has "only limited capacity" to buy back bonds. Bessent told CNBC on Thursday that his team was going to "make a market" and that the purchases would likely top $4 billion.

The U.S. is spending about 40% more than it's bringing in, resulting in the burgeoning budget deficit, Dalio said. The U.S. budget deficit topped $432 billion in July, but Bessent told CNBC that it's likely peaked under President Donald Trump's administration.

Bessent said in the interview that a team was looking at ways to shrink spending by hundreds of billions of dollars. But Dalio said there is "very little ability" to do so given that it is either committed or considered essential.

After years of overspending, Dalio said total debt now dwarfs what the U.S. brings in each year. If the U.S. government was a business, Dalio said debt service payments would come in at roughly $11 trillion — about 200% of annual revenue.

Dalio, 77, warned the cost of repaying principal and servicing the debt will only grow over time.

To solve this, Dalio said the U.S. needs to carefully employ a three-part strategy to get the budget deficit down to 3% of gross domestic product.

First, Dalio said the U.S. government needs to reduce its spending. Second, he said tax revenue needs to be raised. Finally, the New York native and Harvard Business School alumni said lower interest rates were needed.

"All three need to happen concurrently so as to prevent any one from being too large," Dalio said. "If any one is too large, the adjustment will be traumatic."

Dalio cautioned against ramming through these adjustments by "force." For instance, "it would be very bad if the Federal Reserve unnaturally forced interest rates down," he said.

Dalio said it's important to to take steps now, while the economy is healthy. An economy in recession requires increased government spending, he said.

Dalio said the exact timing of a debt crisis can be swayed by variables ranging from military conflict to political change. On its current trajectory, the U.S. could enter such a crisis in as early as one year or as late as five. "My guess, which I suppose will be a bad one, is that it will come in three years, give or take two, if the course we're on is not changed."

To prepare, Dalio recommended investors are underweight debt assets, such as bonds.

As much as 10% to 15% of a portfolio could land in gold, as well as "a bit" of bitcoin, the hedge fund founder said.

Dalio's post came at the tail end of a volatile week for U.S. financial markets. Rising long-term Treasury yields have pressured stocks, leading the S&P 500 to snap a a three-week-long advance.

#3013. UiPath vs. Palantir Technologies: Comparing Modest Revenue Growth vs. Rapid Expansion

Palantir's quarterly revenue has more than doubled over the past eight quarters, while UiPath's has stayed largely flat with recurring seasonal swings.

By Robert Izquierdo – Nov 18, 2026 at 7:25PM EST

UiPath (PATH -5.33%) primarily generates revenue by delivering an integrated suite of enterprise software applications that empowers corporate entities, financial institutions, and government organizations to independently design, deploy, evaluate, and continuously manage automated business processes across their operations.

It recently reorganized its executive leadership team, formally appointing a new chief financial officer, and simultaneously introduced several advanced developer tools specifically designed to help large global enterprises dynamically map and orchestrate their various internal organizational workflows.

Palantir Technologies (PLTR +0.07%) primarily earns revenue by engineering and deploying specialized software systems that enable government intelligence agencies, allied military branches, and large commercial enterprises to integrate, analyze, and gain insights from massive amounts of fragmented information.

It recently secured a major tactical ground station production agreement with the United States Army, while simultaneously establishing multiple new commercial partnerships explicitly aimed at supporting both its global international expansion and its ongoing modular data center infrastructure initiatives.

Revenue represents the total amount of money a business brings in from its core operations before any expenses are subtracted, serving as a fundamental baseline measure of overall customer demand and business growth..

UiPath vs. Palantir Technologies Revenue over the last 8 quarters.

Data source: Financial Modeling Prep. Data as of Nov. 17, 2026.

UiPath and Palantir's revenue trajectories illustrate their sharp divergence in enterprise artificial intelligence adoption. Palantir is experiencing compounding hyper-growth fueled by broad commercial adoption of its artificial intelligence platform (AIP), whereas UiPath has stabilized into moderate, seasonally cyclical expansion.

This distinct difference in sales trends reveals the massive customer exuberance for Palantir's AIP solution, as demonstrated by the consistent quarter-over-quarter revenue growth. Meanwhile UiPath's AI offerings are not finding as much market demand despite the AI boom, with sales volatility from quarter to quarter, although the company is still maintaining year-over-year increases.

That said, UiPath's growth appears to be decelerating. Its fiscal second quarter revenue of $410.3 million was a 13% year-over-year jump, but down from the 17% growth seen in Q1. This decline extends into its fiscal Q3, with the company forecasting sales of at least $440 million, which is about a 7% increase over 2025's $411 million, raising concerns as to whether the company can continue to grow over the long term.

Palantir's soaring sales are due to AIP attracting commercial customers, and successfully expanding beyond its government roots. The company's $2.3 billion in Q3 marked the first time its U.S. commercial sales surpassed U.S. government revenue, signaling a key inflection point in its business.

The new additions bring Chase’s total number of transfer partners to 16.

Chase is growing its roster of airline transfer partners to include Qantas Frequent Flyer and Cathay Pacific Asia Miles.

That’ll bump the number of Chase’s transfer partners to 16, with 12 airline and four hotel loyalty programs. The new partners, which are live as of Nov. 18, 2026, will transfer at a 1:1 ratio from Chase Ultimate Rewards®.

You’ll need an eligible Chase card, such as the Chase Sapphire Reserve® and the Chase Sapphire Preferred® Card, to transfer points.

Qantas and Cathay Pacific are the flag carriers for Australia and Hong Kong, respectively.

Both airlines are part of the Oneworld alliance, which includes U.S. carriers like American Airlines, Alaska Airlines and Hawaiian Airlines. They also maintain relationships with non-alliance partners.

Your best shot at award seats on Qantas and Cathay Pacific flights is often through their own programs. That’s because airlines typically release the most award availability to their own loyalty program members.

While some partner airline programs might offer cheaper award pricing, it can be harder to find award availability through them. That’s why having points and miles with the airline you want to fly on can be so valuable.

This is especially helpful for airlines that operate highly sought-after routes or premium cabin products. For example, Cathay Pacific's Aria Suite in business class is a popular choice for flyers. For example, Cathay Pacific’s Aria Suite in business class is a popular choice for flyers and is only available on select routes.

Meanwhile, Qantas is expected to start flying the world's longest commercial route in October 2027 between Sydney and London, as part of its Project Sunrise initiative. It’s also slated to launch nonstop flights between Sydney and New York the following year.

Those seats aren’t on sale yet, but they’re likely to be highly coveted for award travelers.

These additions aren’t particularly exciting if you already have access to other transferable currencies like Capital One miles, which transfer 1:1 to both Qantas and Cathay Pacific.

#3011. Montauk Renewables: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Swine waste upgrades, roughly $15 million and three months out, will test whether the project timeline holds for investors.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 18, 2026 at 7:27PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Montauk Renewables ›

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 16, 2026, Montauk Renewables (MNTK +3.60%) reaffirmed its full-year production outlook while seeking credit facility amendments to manage liquidity constraints. While operational delays at its North Carolina swine waste facility create short-term debt covenant risks, long-term performance hinges on scaling agricultural renewable energy projects and expanding distribution partnerships. Three things management said stand out.

Engineering delays at the North Carolina project created potential debt covenant non-compliance, forcing management to negotiate credit relief and defer development spending.

Feedstock moisture control and temperature preservation challenges at Montauk Ag Renewables, the company's North Carolina swine waste project, pushed back commercial production schedules and raised capital needs.

Monetizing Renewable Identification Numbers (RINs), which are tradable compliance credits used in renewable fuel markets, through GreenWave helped boost quarterly revenue alongside core renewable natural gas (RNG) operations.

Core landfill operations remain steady, with annual output measured in million British thermal units (MMBtu), as management navigates market pricing shifts in environmental attributes.

Long-term agreements across hog farming locations and specialized fuel dispensing access provide competitive advantages, while management prioritizes liquidity discipline and project execution.

#3010. Elauwit: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Property owners pay wholesale rates and charge tenants below retail, and a 12-month lease rollover builds the recurring base over time.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 18, 2026 at 7:27PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Elauwit Connection ›

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 16, 2026, Elauwit (ELWT +0.01%) announced that monthly recurring revenue, or predictable subscription income, reached $539,000 at quarter-end. That growth matters to long-term investors because recurring subscription revenue creates high-margin cash flow after initial network setup. Three things management said stand out.

Elauwit signs five-to-eight-year contracts with property owners to provide managed Wi-Fi networks across entire apartment communities. Once installation finishes, resident leases roll over onto the network over about 12 months, creating a steady stream of recurring subscription fees.

Property owners purchase internet at wholesale rates and charge tenants a technology fee below typical retail prices. The resulting spread increases net operating income (NOI), the property profit before debt and taxes, which raises total property market value.

As Elauwit expands its footprint across 31 states and Washington D.C., concentration in key cities enables hiring local employees instead of third-party contractors. That shift cuts operational expenses while accelerating technical response times for property managers.

Real estate investment trusts (REITs), entities that own income-producing property, are adopting managed Wi-Fi quickly, and Elauwit is winning portfolio deals through its land-and-expand strategy. Management is building local market density to lower service costs and support margins.

Contracted networks become long-lived recurring revenue once activated. Building owner relationships remain central to expanding recurring revenue.

#3009. Top Wall Street analysts believe in the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Geopolitical uncertainty in the Middle East and soaring bond yields weighed on global markets this past week. And investors are closely assessing the valuations of artificial intelligence stocks amid concerns about the sustainability of high spending on AI infrastructure.

Despite those continued challenges, top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the prospects of several stocks due to their long-run growth potential and exposure to lucrative business lines, including AI.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Cybersecurity company CrowdStrike Holdings (CRWD) is this week's first stock pick. The company is scheduled to announce fiscal second-quarter results on August 26.

Ahead of Q2 FY27 earnings, Truist analyst Junaid Siddiqui reiterated a buy rating on CrowdStrike stock and raised his price target to $245 from $187.50. Following CrowdStrike's impressive fiscal first-quarter performance and guidance raise, Siddiqui expects another strong quarter.

The 5-star analyst's optimism is backed by continued strength in Falcon Flex adoption and encouraging large deal activity. Siddiqui also cited continued momentum across emerging products. With the FY27 outlook assuming persistent strength in CrowdStrike's net new annual recurring revenue (ARR), Siddiqui is more focused on the longevity of key growth catalysts such as Falcon Flex adoption, platform consolidation and AI-led demand.

The analyst highlighted that talks with partners were encouraging, with pipeline generation and momentum in large deals remaining healthy in the fiscal second quarter despite tough, year-over-year comparisons.

"We continue to view CrowdStrike as one of the primary beneficiaries of platform consolidation, though we expect investors to remain focused on competitive dynamics and whether current growth levels represent a new baseline or a period of demand acceleration," the analyst said.

Siddiqui ranks No. 226 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 80% of the time, delivering an average return of about 40%. See CrowdStrike Financials on TipRanks.

Shares of personal computer maker Dell Technologies (DELL) have rallied 251% this year, driven by strong demand for AI servers. Recently, Evercore analyst Amit Daryanani reiterated a buy rating on DELL and raised his price target to $550 from $500.

The 5-star analyst contends that while Dell's AI compute segment is getting investors' attention, the company's Storage business is underappreciated and well-positioned to drive revenue and profit acceleration. Daryanani expects the storage business to see higher demand once enterprises start to bring workload back on-premises.

The analyst highlighted that Dell's Storage business, estimated to account for only 10% of FY27 revenue, is one of the factors that could help improve profit margins, given its higher profitability compared to AI servers. Daryanani estimates that the Storage business's operating margin is currently in the low-20% range, while margins for the AI server and traditional server businesses are in the mid-single-digit and mid-teens range, respectively.

Overall, Daryanani views the Storage business as an important catalyst for three reasons: Dell has one of the broadest storage portfolios in the industry; Storage has a significantly higher margin profile than AI servers; and the shift toward proprietary, software-defined storage and AI-focused platforms.

"We think DELL's storage business is becoming increasingly strategic as AI infrastructure deployments move beyond GPU procurement and toward full stack + AI-ready architectures," said Daryanani.

Daryanani ranks No. 19 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 38.70%. See Dell Technologies Statistics on TipRanks.

SanDisk (SNDK) is benefiting from AI-driven demand for its memory and storage products. Following the company's 2026 Investor Day event, J.P. Morgan analyst Harlan Sur resumed research coverage of SNDK with a buy rating (it was earlier, "Not Rated") and a price target of $2,250.

The 5-star analyst believes SanDisk is well-positioned to capitalize on a structural inflection reached in NAND demand, driven by strong growth in AI inference. Sur's bullishness is based on three key factors.

First, Sur believes that SanDisk's New Business Model (NBM) framework (or long-term agreements) has enhanced its margin profile and reduced cyclicality. SNDK's eight signed NBMs represent a total contract value of $94 billion and a gross margin of about 80%, even at the floor pricing.

Second, Sur highlighted the growing NAND market and expects the total addressable market to surge from about $70 billion in calendar year 2025 to over $300 billion in 2026 and about $500 billion in 2027. Importantly, the analyst noted that data centers are the largest driver of NAND growth, as hyperscalers increasingly deploy flash for AI inference workloads.

Finally, Sur is optimistic on SanDisk due to its technology dominance. The company's BiCS10 technology is sampling ahead of schedule and has 65% more bits per wafer than BiCS8, the analyst said. Also, Sur expects SanDisk's High Bandwidth Flash to become a differentiated memory platform for AI inference.

"Layered on top, mgmt's commitment to returning 100% of excess cash flow to shareholders should drive an aggressive share count reduction trajectory that further amplifies EPS growth on a per-share basis," said Sur.

Sur ranks No. 15 among more than 12,400 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 71% of the time, delivering an average return of 43.20%. See SanDisk Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#3008. World of Hyatt Adds Milestone Rewards, New Delta Benefits

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site or when you click to or take an action on their website. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Hyatt is updating its rewards program in 2027, and it means you'll earn Milestone Rewards even sooner than before.

Editor/Content Strategist & Smart Travel Podcast Host

It’s going to be easier to rack up extra benefits if you’re a frequent Hyatt customer in 2027. World of Hyatt announced new Milestone Rewards, the ability to stack awards (such as Free Night Certificates and Suite Upgrade Awards) on a single stay and a simpler 365-day expiration on all awards.

The news expands on the fresh details revealed Nov. 17, 2026, around World of Hyatt’s partnership with Delta SkyMiles, which was first announced in September.

The additions to the Milestone Rewards are designed to give members more choice and personalization, according to Laurie Blair, senior vice president of global marketing and loyalty at Hyatt.

"We’re shaping the future of World of Hyatt around what our members value most. After a year of listening, one thing is clear: members want more distinctive benefits, more freedom to use their awards and the opportunity to choose awards that matter most to them,” Blair said in an email to NerdWallet.

But top-tier Globalist members will soon have to choose between perks that they previously received automatically. Most notably, Globalists will no longer receive free parking on award nights in 2027. Instead, they can choose a Free Parking Award as a Milestone Reward that they can apply to award or paid stays.

The Delta Air Lines and Hyatt partnership will also mainly benefit frequent flyers and hotel guests. With the exception of the limited-time intro promotion, only Delta or Hyatt members with elite status will earn points in both programs. Hyatt elites will have to purchase premium tickets on Delta — not economy — to earn Hyatt points on the flight.

Starting Jan. 1, 2027, World of Hyatt will extend Milestone Rewards to guests who have not yet qualified for the first level of elite status. Southwest Airlines did the same in its recent revamp to give loyal customers a thank you before they’ve reached any level of elite status.

World of Hyatt members will reach the first Milestone Reward at just 10 nights or 20,000 Base Points. Previously, it was 20 nights or 35,000 Base Points.

The reward choices at the first milestone are:

A tier-qualifying night credit, which gets you closer to the next level of elite status.

A $25 FIND Experience credit (Hyatt’s platform for local experiences such as cooking classes).

A $30 spa credit good at Hyatt Inclusive Collection resorts.

The latter two will likely still require you to pay money out of pocket to use them, as the credit will only cover a portion of your bill. For example, the cheapest spa treatment at Secrets Playa Mujeres Golf & Spa Resort in Cancun, Mexico, will still run you $109 (or $79 after the credit).

The Milestone Rewards start to get good at the higher tiers. Starting at 20 nights, you can select from the existing Milestone Rewards, as well as new items. Some of the new options include:

Free parking on award nights is going away as a Globalist tier benefit. In 2027, a Free Parking Award will be an option that Globalists can select at the 60, 70, 80 and 90 nights The parking award can be used on either a qualifying award or paid stay for up to seven nights.

Hyatt said in a call with NerdWallet that roughly 70% of Globalists weren’t using the free parking benefit at all.

Globalists who have earned status in 2026 for 2027 will be grandfathered into the automatic free parking on award stays through their tier expiration date in February 2028.

To use these new awards, the Hyatt booking process is also getting an upgrade. Previously, awards like Suite Upgrade Awards and Free Night Awards couldn’t be redeemed on the same stay. Starting in 2027, members can combine eligible awards on a single reservation, including Suite Upgrade Awards, Guest of Honor Awards and Free Parking Awards.

The new booking system will also make it easier to pay for some nights of a single reservation with awards, points, cash or a combination of points and cash.

Every award earned going forward will also carry a 365-day expiration from the date it’s issued, replacing the patchwork of different validity windows. Previously, Suite Upgrade and Guest of Honor Awards expired at “rest of year plus 14 days,” which was just a tad confusing. Meanwhile, Free Night Awards earned through Milestone Rewards were valid for 180 days from the date of issuance, which was not a lot of time to plan a trip.

The Milestone Rewards kick in next year, but the other big change around the Delta partnership is available now.

Hyatt elite members can earn World of Hyatt Bonus Points on eligible Delta flights (Delta Comfort, Premium Select, First Class and Delta One). Basic Economy or standard Main Cabin fares will earn nothing from this partnership at all.

Discoverist members earn 1 Bonus Point per $2 spent.

Explorist, Globalist and Lifetime Globalist members earn 1 Bonus Point per $1 spent.

Delta Medallion members (Silver through Diamond) can earn 1 Delta mile per $1 spent at participating Hyatt properties, on top of their regular Hyatt points.

Even if you don’t have elite status with either program, it’s still worth linking accounts to take advantage of the upcoming bonus point promotions:

Those who link their accounts and complete a qualifying Hyatt stay from Nov. 17 to Dec. 31, 2026, will earn one bonus Tier-Qualifying Night Credit.

A second bonus point offer runs Jan. 4 through Feb. 15, 2027, for members who take their first Delta flight or Hyatt stay after linking their accounts. Members will earn 1,000 Delta SkyMiles after the Hyatt stay or 1,000 Hyatt points after their first flight in a premium cabin on Delta.

Hyatt and Delta are also holding a sweepstakes through Nov. 23 — no points or miles required to enter — for a free three-night trip for two at Alila Mayakoba in Riviera Maya, Mexico, plus two $1,000 Delta gift cards. Linking your accounts before entering gets you a bonus entry.

If you are interested in dual accrual in 2027, there are some routes to elite status through travel credit cards. Hyatt’s entry-level elite tier comes as a benefit of holding the World of Hyatt Credit Card, but Discoverists earn Hyatt points at half the rate of Explorist and Globalist elites on premium Delta fares.

You want a travel credit card that prioritizes what’s important to you. Here are some of the best travel credit cards of 2026:

Flexibility, point transfers and a large bonus: Chase Sapphire Preferred® Card

No annual fee: Wells Fargo Autograph® Card

Flat-rate travel rewards: Capital One Venture Rewards Credit Card
