#3007. 3 Investments to Give a Child This Holiday Season and Leave Alone Until 2040

Which holdings can stay untouched for 14 years without anybody watching them?

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 18, 2026 at 7:38PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Vanguard S&P 500 ETF ›

A few shares of stock can be a better holiday gift than most toys, partly because a child won't think about them for a long time. A gift given this season and left alone until 2040 has around 14 years to compound.

The past 14 years show what that can look like. A $1,000 gift of the Vanguard S&P 500 ETF (VOO -0.28%) at the end of November 2012 would be worth almost $6,700 as I write, with dividends reinvested. The same $1,000 in Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA -1.71%)(BRKB -2.07%) Class B shares would've reached around $6,700, too. In Amazon (AMZN +3.82%), it'd be worth about $20,500.

No one should count on a repeat, especially from Amazon. But these are still the three investments I'd choose for a child now, with the index fund as the anchor. (The shares belong to the child, but gifts like this normally sit in a custodial account an adult manages until the child comes of age.)

The Vanguard S&P 500 ETF holds 506 stocks, pretty much the full S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.23%) index of big U.S. companies. Its expense ratio is 0.03%, or $0.30 each year for every $1,000 invested.

For a gift no one plans to watch, the fund's best feature might be that it updates itself. Companies that shrink tend to fall out of the index and are replaced by growing ones, and the fund handles those trades itself, so its owner doesn't have to.

Of course, that also means the fund tilts heavily toward what's largest today. Information technology stocks were around 37% of it at the end of October, so a long slump in tech could hold it back.

Berkshire Hathaway owns the BNSF railroad, a big utility business, and a lot of manufacturing, service, and retail companies. Its insurers also had almost $179 billion of float (premiums collected before claims are paid) as of Sept. 30.

Showing how steady the non-insurance side has been, Berkshire's railroad, energy, and manufacturing, service, and retailing businesses together earned $7.4 billion in the third quarter, 13% more than a year earlier. Their nine-month earnings rose 13%, too. Insurance underwriting earnings, though, dropped 43% in the quarter, and investment income dipped as well, so total operating earnings declined around 6%.

Berkshire hasn't declared a cash dividend since 1967. Instead, it reinvests in its businesses and buys back shares -- around $14.4 billion worth through September. And it still had more than $350 billion in cash and short-term Treasury bills, over a quarter of its market value.

Of course, Warren Buffett won't make the big calls over the next 14 years. Greg Abel is CEO, and Buffett became chairman emeritus in September. How Abel spends that cash might matter more than anything else.

Amazon offers the most growth of the three. Its third-quarter operating income surged 61% year over year to $28.1 billion, and its operating margin of 13.9% was its highest ever. Its cloud unit, Amazon Web Services (AWS), made up $17.8 billion of that, as sales growth accelerated to 39%.

"There has never been a trillion dollar revenue business, and we may have two of them in the next 5-10 years," CEO Andy Jassy said in Amazon's third-quarter earnings release.

Getting there is expensive. Operating cash flow for the last 12 months climbed 29% to $168.3 billion. But trailing-12-month free cash flow dropped from positive $14.8 billion a year ago to negative $7.6 billion as of June, then to negative $42.4 billion as of September, as artificial intelligence (AI) spending piled up.

In short, Amazon is taking in more cash than ever and spending even more. If that spending earns a weak return, the stock might struggle for a long time.

At about 24 times its 2027 earnings estimate, Amazon stock isn't a bargain. But I think it's a fair price for two big growth engines.

I'd make the index fund the largest part -- maybe half the gift, with the rest split between Berkshire and Amazon. The fund spreads the money among hundreds of businesses, so a setback at either company wouldn't ruin the whole gift.

All three will probably drop together in the next bear market. But a gift put aside for a child is less likely to be sold in a panic, which can matter a lot over 14 years. I'd give all three this season.

#3006. Stock-Split Watch: Is Micron Next?

Micron looks poised for a stock split, especially after Sandisk's recent announcement.

By Marc Guberti – Nov 18, 2026 at 7:43PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Micron Technology ›

Micron Technology (MU -1.00%) may be the next tech stock to perform a stock split. Even after a lengthy correction, it still trades above $700 per share. It's normal for companies to initiate splits when they get closer to $1,000 per share, and investors already saw that unfold when fellow memory stock Sandisk (SNDK -2.64%) announced an 8-for-1 split.

While an 8-for-1 split doesn't make sense for Micron at its current price, a smaller split may make sense in the near future.

Micron's memory chips have become a vital part of artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure, and investors have seen that truth play out in recent financial results. Each time that bears question the likelihood of Micron extending its gains, the company delivers robust earnings that beat expectations and hint at more future growth.

Fiscal 2026 fourth-quarter earnings capture how this story has been playing out. Micron told investors in the previous quarter to expect $50 billion in fiscal 2026 Q4 revenue at the midpoint, but it delivered $54.2 billion instead. Beating guidance helped Micron deliver 379% year-over-year revenue growth, and it told investors that fiscal 2027 will be even better.

If Micron continues to deliver tremendous fundamental growth, its stock could rally and reclaim its all-time high above $1,200 per share. A return to that level further justifies a stock split.

Micron has been securing multiyear deals with key tech companies, making it easier to forecast future revenue. These long-term deals were one of the biggest developments that shattered the bearish narrative about inventory gluts possibly weakening Micron's pricing power in the future.

This type of boom-and-bust cycle has been the norm in the semiconductor industry, but AI demand is still going parabolic. Grand View Research projects a 30.6% compound annual growth rate (CAGR) for the artificial intelligence industry through 2033. That suggests more demand for Micron's memory chips and additional pricing power.

The growth stock trades at a measly 4.5 forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio, giving it a lower valuation than most bank stocks, which are achieving much lower growth rates. It's hard to find a stock that combines Micron's valuation and growth rates, and this one-two punch should ignite a stock rally in the months ahead.

Any rapid acceleration in Micron’s stock price, similar to what investors saw earlier this year, would provide further justification for a stock split. Sandisk initiating its own stock split may prompt Micron to follow suit.

#3004. Why Bullish Stock Crushed it on Wednesday

It topped analyst estimates in its latest reported quarter.

By Eric Volkman – Nov 18, 2026 at 7:43PM EST

The investing world was very bullish on cryptocurrency exchange operator Bullish (BLSH +5.66%) on Hump Day. That's mainly because the company posted an earnings report featuring a massive leap in headline profitability. Mr. Market rewarded this by sending Bullish's shares up by almost 6% in that trading session.

Before market open, Bullish, perhaps best known for owning the popular crypto news and analysis web portal CoinDesk, reported that its non-IFRS (adjusted) revenue in the third quarter was $84.4 million. That was up a sturdy 10% year over year, and was well above the average analyst estimate of $79.3 million.

On the bottom line, the company's net income swelled to over $576 million ($3.57 per share) from $18.5 million in the third quarter of 2025.

It was the vagaries of the crypto market that juiced Bullish's profitability. The value of its proprietary digital asset holdings flipped from an $11.4 million loss in the year-ago quarter to a $598.4 million gain. Stripping that and other one-time and unique items out of the equation, the company's adjusted net profit still recorded a gain, coming in at $18.7 million ($0.12 per share) from $13.8 million. Analysts were modeling $0.08 per share for the metric.

In the earnings release, Bullish announced the launch of a $300 million share repurchase program. It did not specify an end date for the initiative.

In the release, CEO Tom Farley signaled Bullish's strategy to grow its business via its pending acquisition. In May, the company agreed to acquire shareholder services company Equiniti in a deal valued at $4.2 billion; the transaction is expected to close this coming January. Farley aims to combine that company’s sprawling public-company shareholder registry with Bullish’s platform to offer tokenized stock trading.

I like that Bullish is active and assertive in building out its operations, and it's admirable that management is seeking ways to distinguish it in the crypto services sphere. That said, I'm not convinced tokenized securities will be a powerful motor of growth, so I'd temper my enthusiasm about its third-quarter showing.

TL;DR: Mortgage rates eased today, but borrowing costs are still expensive for many homebuyers.

Mortgage rates are lower today, but recent averages above 7% are still eating into buyers’ budgets.

The average interest rate on a 30-year, fixed-rate mortgage dropped to 7.13% APR, according to rates provided to NerdWallet by Zillow. This is 21 basis points lower than yesterday and 13 basis points higher than a week ago. (A basis point is one one-hundredth of a percentage point.)

Here are the average APRs we're seeing today, as well as movement over the past month for context:

» Take the next step: Compare mortgage rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

Mortgage rates are constantly changing, since a major part of how rates are set depends on reactions to new inflation reports, job numbers, Fed meetings, global news ... you name it. For example, even tiny changes in the bond market can shift mortgage pricing.

On Friday, the Bureau of Labor Statistics released the Producer Price Index for October, which tracks the change in prices that U.S. producers are able to get for their products. This is one way to measure inflation on the market end, rather than the consumer end.

The results were mixed. While prices didn’t rise quite as high as economists anticipated month-over-month (0.3% vs. 0.4%), year-over-year price growth did reach 6.5% — the largest increase in four years. This points to even higher costs for consumers coming in the near future.

Investors are convinced that the Federal Reserve will raise overnight borrowing rates again in December to pull inflation down. And higher overnight borrowing rates — called the federal funds rate, which is what banks pay to fund products like mortgages — usually lead to higher mortgage rates.

» MORE: How the Federal Reserve affects mortgage rates

Refinancing might make sense if today’s rates are at least 0.5 to 0.75 of a percentage point lower than your current rate (and if you plan to stay in your home long enough to break even on closing costs).

With rates where they are right now, you may want to start considering a refi if your current rate is around 7.63% or higher.

Also consider your goals: Are you trying to lower your monthly payment, shorten your loan term or turn home equity into cash? For example, you might be more comfortable with paying a higher rate for a cash-out refinance than you would for a rate-and-term refinance, so long as the overall costs are lower than if you kept your original mortgage and added a HELOC or home equity loan.

If you're looking for a lower rate, use NerdWallet's refinance calculator to estimate savings and understand how long it would take to break even on the costs of refinancing.

» Time to refi? Compare refinance rates from NerdWallet’s top lenders

There is no universal “right” time to start shopping — what matters is whether you can comfortably afford a mortgage now at today’s rates.

If the answer is yes, don’t get too hung up on whether you could be missing out on lower rates later; you can refinance down the road. Focus on getting preapproved, comparing lender offers, and understanding what monthly payment works for your budget.

NerdWallet’s affordability calculator can help you estimate your potential monthly payment. If a new home isn’t in the cards right now, there are still things you can do to strengthen your buyer profile. Take this time to pay down existing debts and build your down payment savings. Not only will this free up more cash flow for a future mortgage payment, it can also get you a better interest rate when you’re ready to buy.

» Is now a good time to buy? See NerdWallet’s analysis

If you already have a quote you’re happy with, you should consider locking your mortgage rate, especially if your lender offers a float-down option. A float-down lets you take advantage of a better rate if the market drops during your lock period.

Rate locks protect you from increases while your loan is processed, and with the market forever bouncing around, that peace of mind can be worth it.

#2998. Dividend Stocks for 2027: Kimberly-Clark vs. McDonald's

Kimberly-Clark trades at a noticeably lower multiple than McDonald's, but the franchise giant's richer price tag raises a question about which premium is justified.

By Jack Delaney – Updated Nov 18, 2026 at 8:00PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Kimberly-Clark ›

Investors often seek stability in market giants that dominate daily life. Deciding between a consumer essentials powerhouse like Kimberly-Clark (KMB -0.84%) and a global restaurant titan like McDonald's (MCD -0.65%) requires weighing income against growth.

Kimberly-Clark provides essential hygiene products that consumers buy regardless of the economic climate. McDonald's leverages a massive franchise model to generate consistent cash flow through the fast-food market. Comparing these two involves looking at how defensive positioning in household goods stacks up against the high-margin, scalable nature of the global restaurant business.

Kimberly-Clark sells essential hygiene and health products to households and professionals among consumer staples stocks worldwide. Its brands, including Huggies and Kleenex, reach customers in more than 175 countries through supermarkets and e-commerce platforms. Walmart (WMT -0.13%) is its largest customer, accounting for nearly 16% of net sales, and customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached nearly $16.6 billion. The company reported net income of close to $3.3 billion during this same timeframe. Kimberly-Clark has total cash of $1.8 billion, with total debt of $6.5 billion. Over a 12-month trailing period, it has operating cash flow of $3.3 billion.

McDonald's operates a global fast-food empire by primarily franchising its locations to independent owners rather than running them directly. This model allows for high net margins compared to many traditional retail-focused businesses. It also maintains a delivery partnership with Uber Technologies (UBER -0.07%) to help boost volume and customer loyalty across more than a dozen global markets.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached approximately $27.8 billion, which shows revenue growth of nearly 5.8%. Net income for this period was roughly $9 billion, demonstrating the company's ability to turn sales into significant bottom-line profit. The steady expansion of the franchise system and delivery infrastructure supports these consistent financial results.

Kimberly-Clark faces intense competition from other branded household products and lower-priced private label manufacturers. It is sensitive to price changes in volatile commodities like cellulose fiber and petroleum-based materials used in manufacturing. Additionally, the company must manage the integration of its acquisition of Kenvue while navigating global regulatory and sustainability reporting requirements.

McDonald's deals with fierce competition and depends heavily on the financial health of its individual franchisees. Labor shortages and rising wage costs can pressure the system, while food safety concerns or public perception issues can damage the global brand reputation.

McDonald's carries a significantly higher premium than Kimberly-Clark based on both its forward price-to-earnings (P/E) and its price-to-sales (P/S) ratio, which measures market cap relative to sales over the past twelve months.

Both Kimberly-Clark and McDonald's are well-run companies, and while McDonald's is known for its singular brand, Kimberly-Clark is known for multiple brands, ranging from Kleenex to Band-Aid. Both are also dividend stocks, helping investors generate more income. Kimberly-Clark's dividend offers a higher yield of 4.7%, while McDonald's is 2.8%.

That said, between the two, I'd rather own Kimberly-Clark. That's because in tight economic times, Kimberly-Clark offers items people will keep buying, whereas people may limit how often they go out to eat. We are seeing a bit of that play out as McDonald's figures out how to keep prices lower amid rising costs. For its 2026 third-quarter results, restaurant sales in the United States decreased by 0.2%.

In comparison, Kimberly-Clark has more predictable demand, which is nice to have with all the uncertainty swirling around the markets. In the first nine months of 2026, Kimberly-Clark reported a 1% increase in net sales to $12.4 billion. That 1% increase may not sound exciting, but again, it's about consistency. Also, the acquisition of the consumer healthcare company Kenvue gives Kimberly-Clark even more essential products to sell, adding brands like Tylenol and Listerine to its portfolio. While consumers may cut back on going out to eat and ordering burgers and fries, they won't hesitate to buy Huggies or Tylenol if they need them.

#2994. The 'debasement trade' returns after Bessent bond maneuver. Crypto and gold are back in style

The debasement trade is gaining new traction on Wall Street as concern over the size and cost of the budget deficit mounts.

The conventional wisdom behind the trade is that perceived hard assets, like cryptocurrencies and precious metals, gain as investors try to hedge against a weaker U.S. dollar and Treasury debt in the face of ballooning government spending. Those fears reached a fever pitch last week before, and after, the Treasury Department's unusual step of increasing debt buybacks under Secretary Scott Bessent.

"The size of the Treasury purchases announced so far by Bessent are trivial in comparison to the size of the overall market, but the [signaling] effect was very powerful," said Stephen Coltman, head of macro at 21Shares, a crypto-focused creator of exchange-traded funds.

Gold touched three-month highs Monday, building on last week's advance of more than 5%. The yellow metal has climbed for five straight weeks and in August is on track for its biggest monthly rise since 1999.

Bitcoin added 2% Monday, to the highest since May. Last week, the digital currency soared 22%, its biggest three-day rally since 2023. Overnight Tuesday, the crypto touched $80,000.

The U.S. dollar index, which tracks the dollar against six other leading currencies, hit three-month lows last week and recorded its third down week in the last four. The index was little changed on Monday as investors expressed little appetite to bid up the greenback.

The Treasury Department said last week that it would double the maximum size of its bond buyback, to at least $4 billion from $2 billion. Two senior Treasury officials told CNBC Monday that the department could use its General Account to help fund the plans.

Last week's announcement followed news that the monthly U.S. budget deficit in July reached a five-year high and came at the same time as total federal government debt topped $40 trillion. Bessent told CNBC last week that he wields a "big toolkit" to calm the government bond market amid concerns about the government's financial health.

Long-dated U.S. Treasury yields surged last week, at one point sending the 30-year yield to almost a 20-year high of 5.34%, up from 4.82% in late June. Yields dipped and then rebounded in the wake of the Treasury buyback move, a signal that bond investors believed Bessent's moves were inadequate.

"Markets are saying something," the billionaire philanthropist and former energy trader John Arnold said in a Friday post on X. The weaker dollar, lower Treasury prices and strengthening hard assets, are "all part of the debasement trade," he wrote.

The Treasury's moves could aid the bond market, but also translate into outsized pain for the dollar, according to Nohshad Shah, Citadel Securities' head of fixed income sales for Europe, the Middle East and Africa.

The stakes are high for monetary policymakers, as a weaker greenback can ease financial conditions, Shah said. U.S. inflation — above the Federal Reserve's 2% target for five years — could worsen.

The Fed might need to hike interest rates in response, Shah said. Fed funds futures reflect about a 56% chance the central bank raises borrowing costs at its October meeting, up more than seven percentage points from a week ago, according to CME's FedWatch tool.

"The bond market's message is straightforward: fiscal or monetary policy should be tighter," Shah said in a Monday note. "Households may ultimately pay for policymakers' unwillingness to fix the roof whilst the sun is shining."

Coltman at 21Shares said the appetite for alternative stores of value also stems from mounting geopolitical tension. The U.S. on Monday rolled out a global sanctions program focused on segregating Iran from the global economy, days after slapping 50% tariffs on billions of dollars in Canadian exports.

Still, assets that would benefit from a debasement trade have recently received votes of confidence on Wall Street.

Deutsche Bank analyst Michael Hsueh said in a Monday note that gold could surpass his target price of $4,800 an ounce. Already, that level suggests the precious metal only need to rise another 3% or so from where it closed on Friday. "We see the Treasury policy change as underlining the gold constructive view," Hsueh said.

Billionaire investor Ray Dalio recommended investors remain overweight gold and bitcoin ahead of what he warned could become a debt crisis in the U.S. as a result of unchecked government spending. Gold could account for as much as 15% of a model portfolio, the Bridgewater Associates founder said.

"The government's financial condition is at an inflection point," Dalio wrote on LinkedIn Friday. "If this is not dealt with now, the debts will build up to levels where they can't be managed without great trauma."

Others are less sure, devaluing the notion of a debasement trade.

It's probably too early to endorse the idea unless it's clear that the Fed will go along with the Treasury Department, said Alexander Lis, investing chief at Social Discovery Ventures.

#2992. Warren Buffett Expects Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA, BRKB) Stock to Drop About 50% Again Someday. Here's What He Says to Do Then.

Berkshire's stock has already been roughly halved (or more) three times. That should give investors confidence.

By Selena Maranjian – Nov 18, 2026 at 8:05PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Berkshire Hathaway ›

When Warren Buffett talks, most savvy investors listen. Last year, in his now-annual Thanksgiving letter, he said:

Our stock price will move capriciously, occasionally falling 50% or so as has happened three times in 60 years under present management.

Buffett followed that sentence with this one:

Don't despair; America will come back and so will Berkshire shares.

Buffett was at the helm of Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA -1.71%) (BRKB -2.07%) for about 60 years, and grew the company by a remarkable annual average of nearly 20% over that period. Now 96 years old, he has since stepped away from that top job and is chairman emeritus.

Buffett actually detailed the three 50%-ish drops earlier, in his 2017 letter to shareholders, along with a 37% drop. I'm including that data in the table below. Note that it refers to the company's Class A shares, not the Class B shares that most shareholders likely own.

Both classes of stock generally move in tandem. As I write this, Class A shares are trading at around $878,500 apiece, and Class B shares near $587. Few of us could afford a share of Class A stock, which is part of the reason that Class B shares exist.

There are lessons to be drawn from that table that apply to much of our investing, not just to Berkshire Hathaway stock. For starters, note how the lengths of the declines vary -- from a few weeks to a year or two.

That's a great reminder that whenever the stock market corrects or crashes -- and the S&P 500 has actually been generally declining over the past month or so, down about 3% -- it may not stay down very long. (Berkshire Class A shares, meanwhile, are up about 17.5% over the past month!)

Note, too, that Berkshire shares and stocks, in general, tend to bounce back quite well, either quickly or eventually. Look at that 50.7% drop nearly 20 years ago, from $147,000 per share to $72,400. And look where the shares are now -- $878,500.

The folks at The Hartford Funds have offered some handy insights about market downturns, noting that:

So take Buffett's advice to heart. Expect occasional downturns and plan to ride them out while seeking bargains. Only keep money in stocks that you won't need for five, if not more, years, so that you can ride out a downturn.

#2991. Top Wall Street analysts suggest these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Inflation data, earnings reports and tensions in the Middle East have been influencing the market. In this volatile backdrop, investors seeking income can bolster their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

The recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help inform investors in their search for attractive stocks from the vast universe of dividend-paying companies. These experts assign ratings after analyzing a company's financials and ability to consistently pay dividends while also seeking capital appreciation.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Exxon Mobil has increased its dividends for 43 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.03 per share (annualized dividend of $4.12), XOM stock offers a yield of 2.6%.

In his latest research report on energy players, Morgan Stanley analyst Devin McDermott reiterated a buy rating on Exxon Mobil stock and increased his price target to $177 from $168.

The analyst raised his free cash flow estimates to reflect Q2 guidance and the oil price strip, with the largest increase noted for integrated players due to higher refining margins. Notably, the five-star analyst raised his cash flow estimates by an average of 20% for the second half of 2026 and 7% for 2027.

McDermott highlighted that while pure-play refiners have rallied on stronger margins, integrated energy stocks have lagged, creating relative-value opportunities in major North American integrated producers, including Exxon Mobil, Suncor and Cenovus Energy.

Overall, McDermott is bullish on Exxon Mobil, as he expects the company to deliver strong earnings and cash flow growth, driven by robust upstream volumes, high-value projects in Product Solutions, and more than $5 billion of additional structural cost savings.

"XOM's large global integrated operations and strong balance sheet (0.0x 2027 net leverage) make it a clear defensive play in an uncertain macro backdrop," said McDermott.

McDermott ranks No. 726 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 9.80%. See Exxon Mobil Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Moving on to natural gas producer Expand Energy. The company announced a quarterly base dividend of $0.575 per share to be paid on Sept. 3. At an annualized dividend of $2.30 per share, EXE offers a yield of about 2.3%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reaffirmed a buy rating on Expand Energy stock and increased his price target to $113 from $99. Mehta attributed his higher price target to cash flow improvement and a strong outlook for shareholder returns. He also cited the stock's attractive valuation, with an 11% free cash flow, or FCF, yield based on his 2027/2028 estimates compared with the peer average of 9%.

The five-star analyst expects Expand Energy to generate FCF per share of about $10 in FY28, up from the prior estimate of about $8 at his mid-cycle price forecast of $3.50/MMBtu Henry Hub. Mehta raised his FCF estimate to reflect an improved outlook for share repurchases, operational efficiencies, and gas pricing realizations.

Furthermore, the analyst expects the recently announced $1.25 billion Twin Eagle acquisition to enhance the competitive positioning of the combined business to expand to premium markets and capture incremental deals in the power and LNG end markets.

Mehta ranks No. 449 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.1%. See Expand Energy Insider Trading on TipRanks.

Mehta is also bullish on Diamondback Energy, an independent oil and natural gas company focused on energy reserves that are primarily in the Permian Basin in West Texas. The company recently paid a base cash dividend of $1.10 per share for the second quarter of 2026. FANG stock offers a dividend yield of 2.2%.

Mehta increased his price target on FANG stock to $220 from $212 and reiterated a buy rating, citing the company's capital-efficient volume growth as oil prices remain high amid persistent supply disruption in the Middle East.

"We remain constructive on FANG, a high-quality, pure-play Permian Basin operator that continues to drive incremental capital efficiencies and strong well productivity relative to peers," said Mehta.

Also, the analyst said that the removal of a minimum return of capital commitment gives Diamondback more flexibility in taking capital allocation decisions amid high oil price volatility and macro uncertainty.

Mehta added that Diamondback sees a favorable environment for production growth, driven by the potential need for global inventory restocking. He highlighted that during Q2 2026, FANG's production volumes of 1,018 Mboe/d exceeded the high end of the company's guidance, driven by a stronger-than-expected natural gas output from Barnett development and improved downstream gas marketing.

Overall, Mehta is optimistic about Diamondback's growth and sees more upside in the stock, given higher production estimates, solid productivity and a deepening position in the Barnett shale formation. See Diamondback Energy Financials on TipRanks.

#2988. What You Can Pay for with Scholarship Money

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Scholarships are usually restricted to the necessary costs of attending college, but some scholarships let you apply the money to more than tuition.

Scholarships are meant to pay for the cost of attendance to study at a college or university. This often includes required bills and purchases, such as tuition, textbooks and on-campus living expenses.

Some scholarships restrict the funds to specific costs, such as tuition. How you can apply the money may depend on the scholarship’s requirements.

Here are the most common college costs you can pay with scholarship money.

Scholarships commonly pay toward the tuition and fees your college charges.

Tuition usually covers only the cost to enroll in classes. Your college will also charge fees for a variety of reasons, such as attending a lab portion of a class, access to on-campus technology, facility maintenance and student activities. You’ll have to pay many of these fees regardless of if you use those facilities or participate in campus activities. Fees are often charged with tuition on the same bill so that you pay them together.

Tuition and fees cost students at public four-year institutions an average of $32,730 for the 2026-2027 school year, according to College Board’s 2026 report. If you use scholarship money to pay for these costs upfront, it can help you save a good chunk of money.

Scholarship money also pays for textbooks and supplies you need to complete your courses. These can include physical books, e-books and software licenses to access online content.

Depending on the scholarship, the cost of additional supplies that are required for certain courses might be covered as well. For example, nursing students might have to purchase scrubs with their college’s logo or medical supplies such as stethoscopes for clinical shifts.

Some scholarships will pay for the purchase of a laptop because you’ll likely need one to complete your coursework; however, you’ll want to confirm that the money can be used for that purchase before buying one.

Your scholarship might help cover some of the costs of living on campus, including paying for a dorm room and meal plans (often called room and board). To apply the funds, you usually have to live in university housing, which might be in a dormitory or even university apartments.

Many students can purchase meal plans to eat meals in their college and university cafeterias. These plans are usually for a set number of meals each week and might be required for first-year students. Because meal plans are part of college expenses, some scholarships will let you apply the funds to purchase a meal plan.

Some scholarships might also allow money to pay for campus parking fees because those are paid directly to the college or university.

» MORE: What is need-based financial aid for college?

Scholarships are generally intended only for educational expenses such as those explained above, but there are some scholarships that want you to use the money for specific expenses. If your scholarship has this type of restriction, your award letter should outline how the funds must be used, such as paying for tuition and fees only.

If you aren’t certain whether there are restrictions on how you can spend your scholarship money and the scholarship was paid directly to your college, you can call your financial aid office to ask. But you’ll want to contact the organization that awarded you the scholarship to ask about restrictions if the scholarship money was sent to you instead.

Even if a scholarship is open to most educational costs, expenses that are rarely allowed to be covered by a scholarship include:

Some transportation costs such as car payments.

Non-essential supplies such as clothing or entertainment.

Scholarships can be paid directly to your college or to you, depending on the individual scholarship. If the money goes to your college, it’s applied to your account based on the scholarship’s requirements. For example, if the funds are only allowed to pay for tuition, the financial aid office will apply the money to your tuition bill to reduce how much you owe.

Scholarships work the same way for room and board. If your scholarship will pay toward the costs of living on campus, the money will reduce how much you owe or cover the costs entirely so that you don’t owe anything.

In some cases, you might be able to use your scholarship money to pay for books or supplies. If that’s allowed, the funds might be available on your account through the college bookstore. Instead of paying the entire cost of your textbooks with cash or your student loans, you can pay with the scholarship money.

If your scholarship sends you the money instead of giving it to your college, you’ll be responsible for using the funds to pay for eligible costs instead of your university automatically applying it toward your bill.

#2987. Why Peabody Energy Stock Slumped Today

An analyst made a deep cut to his price target on the coal company.

By Eric Volkman – Nov 18, 2026 at 8:09PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Peabody Energy ›

Investors struggled to find the energy to be positive about Peabody Energy (BTU -1.45%) on Wednesday. An analyst tracking the storied coal company made a significant cut to his price target, which dampened sentiment toward the stock. Its shares closed the day nearly 1.5% lower.

That move came before the market open and was made by Freedom Broker's Vitaly Kononov. He now believes Peabody stock is destined for $22 per share, down considerably from his former level of $30.

Despite the deep cut, Kononov remained bullish on the coal company's future, as he maintained his buy recommendation.

Centurion is rightfully considered to be Peabody's most important play, so how it develops will make a major difference to the company's results. What I'd be nervous about if I were a company shareholder is the future of the energy industry, an important client base for Peabody's thermal coal business, given Peabody's continued (and deep) involvement in the segment. For numerous reasons, the energy sector has become greener over the years, and I think that's a permanent shift.

I'm bearish on coal companies generally for this reason, and as such I'm bearish on Peabody.

#2982. Anthropic Priced Its IPO at $83. Here's What I'd Pay for the Stock.

Anthropic's explosive growth has made its IPO one of the biggest events in AI. But its enormous valuation could limit investors' future returns.

By Matt DiLallo – Nov 18, 2026 at 8:30PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic, PBC ›

Anthropic's (ANT +0.00%) initial public offering (IPO) is one of the biggest events in the AI industry. The developer of Claude has grown its revenue at an extraordinary pace as businesses adopt its AI tools. The AI giant just priced its IPO at $83 per share, above its expected range of $70 to $78 per share.

However, that price already assumes considerable success. Even if Anthropic becomes one of the world's most successful AI companies, its stock could still deliver disappointing returns from here.

I ran the numbers on what Anthropic's stock could be worth over the next five and 10 years. Here's what they suggest, and the price I'd be willing to pay for the AI stock.

Anthropic recorded $33.2 billion in revenue in just the first nine months of 2026, up a blistering 1,200% compared to the same period last year. Its third-quarter revenue alone was around $17 billion, and its annualized revenue run rate reached $81 billion by Oct. 31, up $10 billion from the prior month.

Enterprise adoption of Claude, its coding tools, API usage, and integrations give Anthropic several avenues for continued growth. However, AI infrastructure and model training are extraordinarily expensive. Anthropic committed to spending a staggering $895.4 billion on compute and infrastructure via purchase commitments and lease agreements. Meanwhile, Anthropic's revenue growth will also almost certainly slow as its business scales.

At $83 per share, Anthropic has a valuation of about $1.5 trillion. That's around 18 times its $81 billion run rate.

That run rate isn't the same as reported annual sales. It extrapolates recent results, which inflates a company growing this quickly. Meanwhile, compute costs, research spending, and future capital needs make it difficult to turn that revenue into sustainable profits. Additionally, stock-based compensation adds dilution, which will be a headwind to per-share earnings growth when the company finally becomes profitable.

The crucial question isn't whether Anthropic is a great company. It's whether its growth can justify a valuation this high.

Using Anthropic competitor, ChatGPT, I modeled three scenarios for Anthropic based on its $83 IPO price. Each one estimates 2031 revenue, net margin, and a price-to-earnings (P/E) multiple, then divides the resulting equity value by a share count that grows 2% a year. Here are my assumptions:

Here's what those assumptions imply for Anthropic's share price:

My base case assumes Anthropic grows its revenue to $400 billion by 2031 with an 18% net margin and a 25x earnings multiple. That would be a remarkable business, yet it would only lift the stock to $91, about 10% above the IPO price, or roughly 2% annually. Meanwhile, my bear case assumes slower growth, thinner margins, and a lower multiple, which would send the shares down roughly 73%. It would take my bull case, with a 24% net margin and a 30x earnings multiple, to deliver a strong return of about 19% annually.

The base case return isn't compelling. Anthropic could execute extremely well and still deliver underwhelming returns because the IPO price already reflects so much success. That's the key takeaway: explosive business growth doesn't guarantee an attractive investment return.

Extending the base case to 2036 improves the outlook, but not by much. Here are the assumptions:

Those assumptions are demanding, as Anthropic would need to double its 2031 revenue and keep widening its margins. Yet the payoff would be only about an 8% annualized return.

I want to earn at least 10% annually from a stock like this using conservative assumptions. For Anthropic's base-case price of $91 in 2031 to deliver that return, I'd need to buy at around $56 per share. That's roughly 30% below the IPO price.

That's why I'd rather wait for a better entry point than chase an elevated first-day price. I'd prefer to buy closer to $55-$60 per share. Even then, I'd want to see that Anthropic can scale its revenue, produce sustainable margins, and defend its position against rival AI platforms.

Anthropic could become one of the most important technology companies of the next decade, and its revenue growth and enterprise adoption are extraordinary. However, the IPO price already reflects that potential, and my base case points to modest returns over the next five years.

I'd rather risk missing the initial excitement than overpay for an exceptional business. The stock gets much more interesting to me at $55 to $60 per share, as long as the underlying growth outlook remains intact.

#2976. I Didn’t Know Southwest Points Could Be This Valuable

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site or when you click to or take an action on their website. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Some of the best Southwest redemptions are only available if you hold one of the airline’s credit cards.

In a world of transferable points currencies, you might not think holding an airline or hotel credit card is necessary. But travel brands are pushing back against that narrative, offering exclusive perks and redemption packages to those who carry their cards.

Take Southwest Airlines, for example. Its Rapid Rewards loyalty program allows you to redeem points for flights, but it also offers a special program called Rapid Rewards Access Events exclusively for Southwest credit cardholders. In exchange for a decent chunk of points, you can get a vacation package that’s worth significantly more than the price you pay. Instead of a traditional flight award redemption, you get a cohesive travel experience.

"Experiences create lifetime memories,” says Nandika Suri, Southwest’s vice president of Rapid Rewards. “For us, we really want them to have great memories and remember our brand as part of that.”

Southwest invited me to participate in one of its Rapid Rewards Access Events. While Southwest covered the cost of my trip, these opinions are my own. Here’s what I learned.

Every event is different (ranging from concerts to multiday trips), but big-ticket Rapid Rewards Access Events tend to include flights, hotel accommodations, meals and excursions.

The event I participated in was in Maui to celebrate the 30th anniversary of Southwest and Chase’s partnership. This was a bigger production than the typical event, with about 60 cardholders (and a plus one) participating. A typical multiday event might have 20 or 25 packages for sale.

In exchange for 275,000 Rapid Rewards points, cardholders received for themselves and one guest:

A four-night stay in an ocean-view room at the Grand Wailea, a Waldorf Astoria Resort, in Maui, including daily breakfast.

Ground transportation between the airport and the resort, as well as for any scheduled group activities.

A special welcome reception and farewell dinner.

A snorkeling cruise with lunch and beverages included.

A choice between several excursions, including a spa treatment, golf, ziplining and more.

Even at extremely conservative estimates, I would value all of those things at roughly $4,600. That’s $750 more than what NerdWallet values 275,000 Rapid Rewards points at, according to our 2026 data. If we took a more liberal approach, I think it would be fair to say the package was worth more than $7,000. That puts this redemption between 1.7 and 2.5 cents per point. (Expand the section below to see how I arrived at those numbers.)

As of 2026, NerdWallet values Southwest Rapid Rewards points at 1.4 cents apiece. That puts the 275,000-point Southwest event package at $3,850.

Let’s put some numbers to the package inclusions and see how the points price stacks up to the cash valuation.

$1,000 Southwest gift card: It’s pretty easy to value a $1,000 Southwest gift card at $1,000. You need to fly to the event, so that can be taken at face value. (You could also use it toward a different trip, if you wanted to use points or fly with a different carrier.)

Four-night hotel stay: For the Grand Wailea stay, a nonrefundable Hilton Honors discount rate for an ocean-view room on the exact same dates in 2027 costs $4,490.19, after factoring in taxes and fees. (The base-level terrace-view room would cost $3,992.17.)

If you were to use points, a standard room without an ocean view would cost 130,000 Hilton points per night. An ocean-view room’s rate shoots up to 281,000 Hilton points per night.

Using NerdWallet’s 2026 valuation of Hilton points at 0.4 cent per point, that puts the value of the two rooms at $2,080 and $4,496, respectively. To make things interesting, let’s say the hotel stay is worth $2,080.

Daily breakfast at the hotel buffet is about $60 per person, before tax and tip. For four days, let’s put that at around another $500, bringing the total stay value to $2,580.

Ground transportation: Let’s make things simple and just say it’s $30 each way between the airport and the resort. For two people, that would be worth $120.

Welcome reception and farewell dinner: Southwest and Chase put a lot of effort into making these events nice, from the food and free-flowing alcohol to the entertainment (there was even a fire performance at the farewell dinner).

I would value this at $50 per person, conservatively, or $200 total.

Snorkeling cruise: I went on Viator to find a similar experience, and the cheapest option I found was priced at $100 per person.

Again, let’s price it conservatively and say it’s worth $200. That price doesn't include hotel transportation, gratuities, towels or alcoholic beverages, while the Southwest package did.

Excursion choice: Southwest let everyone choose an excursion, ranging from a chill pool or spa day to ziplining through the jungle or learning to surf.

It’s hard to put a number on this, because the activities varied in cost.

I went ziplining, and it was $240 for six lines. That price doesn’t include transportation to the excursion, which was covered by Southwest. Meanwhile, a standard massage at the hotel spa is $275 (and that doesn’t even factor in the 20% service charge).

Let’s put this at $250 per person, giving us a round $500 for two people’s excursions.

Grand total: Summing up these numbers gets us $4,600. That’s $750 more than the $3,850 NerdWallet values 275,000 points at, but keep in mind that we probably undervalued several of the inclusions. The actual value of a trip like this could be much higher.

It also doesn’t factor in other things, like snacks provided at the hospitality booth, as well as surprise gifts and goodie bags prepared by Southwest and Chase for attendees.

Before you book a Rapid Rewards Access Event, there are some drawbacks to booking a vacation this way.

The biggest one is if you don’t like group trips or following a schedule on your vacations. Given the nature of these events, you’ll be surrounded by a group of travelers, many of whom will likely be longtime Southwest flyers. If you prefer to keep to yourself, then this type of trip is likely not a good fit.

You’ll also have multiple scheduled activities on your itinerary. While you can choose to not show up, those activities add a lot of value to the trip.

With that said, I found the itinerary quite laid back. The amount of scheduled activities wasn’t overwhelming, and there was plenty of free time to do whatever you wanted. One couple I met even rented a car and explored Maui in their downtime, so they kept themselves busy.

These events are extremely popular, and many attendees I met at the Maui event had been on multiple Southwest cardmember trips.

Scoring a ticket to a Rapid Rewards Access Event might even require setting your alarm for when tickets drop. Several cardmembers told me they’ve experienced times when the event they wanted was sold out in hours, if not minutes. (The Maui one took a few hours to sell out, because it was larger than most.)

Finally, 275,000 points is a lot — even if you're getting great value. If your Rapid Rewards points are collecting dust in your account, then it could make sense. But if you have a transferable currency, then you'd have to weigh whether you might get better value elsewhere, such as booking round-trip business class flights.

Southwest isn’t the only airline or travel brand to offer special events for cardholders. United Airlines has MileagePlus Exclusives and United Card Events from Chase; Delta Air Lines offers cardmember-exclusive redemptions in its SkyMiles Experiences program; and Marriott has its Marriott Bonvoy Moments program.

But every redemption option is different — even if it’s within the same loyalty program. For example, some Rapid Rewards Access Events are packages you can purchase immediately for a set price, while others are auction-style listings. Some programs offer paid experiences, so you can only pay in cash, not points.

Personally, I’m a fan of the fixed-price points option. You know the upfront price, and you don’t have to worry about getting into a last-minute bidding war that could lead you to paying more than you intended. Plus, Southwest has historically had limited award redemption options, although that will change in 2027 when it allows international partner award bookings.

Ultimately, whether you think a Rapid Rewards Access Event is a worthwhile redemption will depend on your interests, and Southwest is working to expand its offerings to meet people’s passions.

"We do offer a lot of breadth because we want to get different types of customers engaged with different types of things based on their individual passion points and what they're really interested in,” says Suri, the Rapid Rewards chief.

If you’d rather spend time in the mountains than on the beach, you might not want to spend 275,000 Rapid Rewards points on the Maui package.

Instead, you probably would've been interested in the 250,000-point package for "Boots, Bourbon and Bonfires" — a three-day trip to Devil's Thumb Ranch, an upscale dude ranch resort about 1.5 hours outside of Denver.

Before you book, just remember: You’ll need a Southwest credit card to purchase.

#2973. Is Anthropic’s IPO a Good Reason to Buy Ginkgo Bioworks Right Now?

The AI developer's offering will give it a pile of cash that it might choose to spend on biomedical research automation.

By Alex Carchidi – Nov 18, 2026 at 8:56PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Ginkgo Bioworks ›

Anthropic (ANT +0.00%) is planning to move forward with its initial public offering (IPO), with trading expected to begin Nov. 19. In doing so, the AI company will raise tens of billions of dollars, which it plans to spend on computing power and infrastructure. But with its focus increasingly turning toward orienting its AI capabilities toward bioscience, the next few years will likely see it make major investments to secure the ingredients it needs to test the hypotheses its AI proposes.

Chief among those ingredients are lab space and biomedical research equipment -- and, as it's a tech company bent on automation, laboratory robotics and other research automation solutions will be near the top of the list.

Enter Ginkgo Bioworks (DNA +1.44%), a lab-automation biotech and aspiring biomanufacturer that's already collaborating with Anthropic's biggest competitor, OpenAI. While many other businesses offer different components of an automated biopharma research stack, Ginkgo says its cart-based system is more modular, expandable, robust, and customizable than any competing platform on the market. So does Anthropic, and its soon-to-land massive pile of money make Ginkgo worth buying right now?

Ginkgo has a unique approach to developing a fully autonomous lab designed to handle multiple types of experiments with minimal hands-on time.

Its solution features a series of robotic carts, each with a central cabinet with an optional small sterile environment. They are connected so they can pass samples and data along after each experiment, while the software plans the next experiments. Humans set the system's goals and check its results. Each cart typically has at least one piece of laboratory hardware in its cabinet, such as a liquid-handling robot, a centrifuge, or an analyzer. Ginkgo's Nebula lab runs more than 100 of these reconfigurable carts.

OpenAI has already put one of its advanced models in charge of a Ginkgo lab. In OpenAI's pilot program, GPT-5 took on the role of AI scientist, designing 36,000 small experiments over a period of six months that Ginkgo's cart-based robots then ran while humans prepped the necessary reagents. Ultimately, the pilot program supposedly reduced the production cost of a test protein by 40%.

In third-quarter 2026, Ginkgo said frontier AI labs also use its cloud lab (lab work customers order remotely) for getting more training data, and it even sold its first autonomous lab to Novo Nordisk (NVO -3.12%). Still, its Q3 revenue fell 6% year over year to $28 million, and it reported a $41 million net loss from continuing operations. One important thing to remember here is that a large portion of early biopharma research is done manually, which means paying labor costs rather than capital expenditures for automation hardware.

Anthropic already has a bioscience laboratory, and it contracts out some of its physical scientific workload when it's efficient to do so. For example, a muscular dystrophy program it recently acquired went on to coordinate experiments across 11 external labs. That's a very high number of collaborators to juggle for a project in preclinical-stage research. Ginkgo could be competing to be one of those collaborators in the future, as contract research organizations (CROs) doubtlessly were and are.

However, Anthropic has no public ties to Ginkgo, unlike OpenAI. Its Model Hardware Standard, which is intended to enable AI agents to operate lab equipment, has eight vendor partners, including a lab-automation business called Automata, but not Ginkgo. Since that system is intended to work with any programmable device, it means rivals’ lab automation hardware would work just as easily as Ginkgo’s, making its capabilities a harder sell.

Anyone wanting upside from Anthropic's biology push can buy its stock once it's listed. It's very possible that Ginkgo will work with Anthropic in the future. As of now, though, the upcoming IPO isn't something to bank on for Ginkgo’s stock, and it doesn’t constitute a distinct reason to buy it yet.

#2969. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 stocks for the long haul

Global stock markets have been volatile as investors assess high bond yields and continued tensions between the U.S. and Iran. There are concerns about the impact of uncertainty in the Middle East on oil prices and consequently on inflation.

Investors who prefer to look beyond short-term noise and buy stocks with attractive long-term growth potential can follow the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts. The ratings of these experts are backed by in-depth research of a company's financials and thorough analysis of the factors affecting its business performance.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Semiconductor giant Nvidia (NVDA) reported stellar fiscal second-quarter results and reassured investors about continued strength in AI-driven demand for its chips. Moreover, Nvidia's FY28 revenue growth outlook of 70% was well above the Street's expectations.

Reacting to the Q2 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reiterated a buy rating on NVDA and increased his price target to $300 from $288. "This is our Top Pick in the semis group, with a compelling product cycle, exceptional growth and valuation below peers," said the 5-star analyst.

Nvidia delivered impressive quarterly results despite supply constraints, Moore said, but he believes that the FY28 revenue and gross margin commentary mattered more, with a favorable outlook for both measures.

Moore said Nvidia's 70% revenue growth guidance for FY28 was above his own estimate of 52% and the consensus of about 40%. The top-line growth estimate was a "remarkable figure" given supply issues and Moore said he expects Nvidia to continue to address bottlenecks to deliver rapid growth.

Among other takeaways, the analyst said performance metrics for Vera Rubin chips were strong, with Nvidia emphasizing a 30x higher throughput per megawatt and 35x lower token cost compared with Grace Blackwell Ultra.

Moore ranks No. 159 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 24.60%. See Nvidia Statistics on TipRanks.

Ride-hailing and delivery platform Uber Technologies (UBER) was recommended by BMO Capital analyst Brian Pitz, who discussed the company's opportunity in the autonomous vehicle (AV) market in a recent research note that reiterated a buy rating and price target of $119.

The 5-star analyst highlighted Uber's transition into a diversified marketplace, with investors now focusing on its AV growth prospects. The key question is how much value can Uber generate as AVs become commercialized, Pitz asked.

"We continue to believe that Uber's AV strategy is evolving and will become a significant driver of revenue and profitability," the analyst said.

Importantly, Pitz believes that Uber can establish itself as a preferred mobility platform for AV makers, supported by its growing AV infrastructure capabilities and an expanding partner base. This would enable Uber to win a significant share of value as the AV market is commercialized.

Additionally, Pitz noted Uber's enhanced position in the AV value chain, with the company shifting from serving as a distribution channel for robotaxis to becoming a broader platform for AV mobility. The analyst discussed Uber's efforts to capture growth opportunities in the AV market, including higher capital spending, expansion into new markets and building partnerships beyond Alphabet's Waymo.

Pitz ranks No. 574 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of about 12.20%. See Uber Technologies Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Semiconductor company Marvell Technology (MRVL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the second quarter of FY27. But the company's increased FY28 guidance fell short of investors' high expectations.

In reaction to the Q2 FY27 results, KeyBanc analyst John Vinh reaffirmed a buy rating on Marvell Technology, with a price target of $400. The analyst noted that Marvell's results and outlook were driven by strength in its data center business. The Q2 FY27 data center business grew 46% year-over-year, to $2.17 billion, far above KeyBanc's estimate of $2.09 billion.

Management "highlighted accelerating AI networking demand across scale-out, scale-across and scale-up architectures, supported by strong 800G optical DSP demand, rapid 1.6T ramp, broader 51.2T switch deployments and higher TIA/driver demand," Vinh said.

Consequently, Marvell raised its data center revenue guidance, now expecting 60% growth in FY27 and more than 60% in FY28.

Regarding the recently announced $120 billion warrant arrangement with Google, Vinh said management argued that programs tied to the warrant are already priced into the custom outlook through FY28. Meanwhile, new programs and those in execution are expected to drive significant upside to the previous FY29 custom XPU revenue estimate of over $10 billion, Vinh said, citing management.

Vinh ranks No. 123 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 60% of the time, delivering an average return of 28.50%. See Marvell Technology Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2968. e.l.f. Beauty vs. On: Which Stock Is the Better Buy for 2027?

One of these brands is growing sales at roughly 40% while the other is trading at a discount to the market.

By John Ballard – Updated Nov 18, 2026 at 9:04PM EST

10 stocks we like better than e.l.f. Beauty ›

Investors often look for disruptive growth stories when evaluating the consumer space. Today we compare e.l.f. Beauty (ELF +2.30%) and On (ONON +1.82%) to determine which stock offers a more compelling opportunity for long-term growth.

e.l.f. Beauty has built a loyal following by offering vegan and cruelty-free cosmetics at accessible prices. Meanwhile, On is a Swiss athletic brand gaining significant market share from legacy footwear giants. Both companies are successfully leveraging digital engagement and strategic retail partnerships to outpace their respective industries.

e.l.f. Beauty operates as a multi-brand beauty company, offering cosmetics and skincare through brands like e.l.f. SKIN and Naturium. It primarily serves value-conscious consumers through major retail stores including Target, Walmart, and Amazon.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached $1.9 billion, representing growth of approximately 40% compared to the prior period. Net income was roughly $87 million over the trailing four quarters.

As of its Sept. 2026 balance sheet, the current ratio is 2.6x, which measures a company's ability to cover short-term liabilities with short-term assets. The debt-to-equity ratio is approximately 0.8x, a metric that compares total debt to shareholder equity to assess financial leverage.

Trailing-12-month free cash flow was $276 million. Note that stock-based compensation accounted for roughly 41% of operating cash flow, inflating reported cash generation, since SBC is a non-cash expense added back to cash flow.

On is a Swiss premium sportswear brand that designs and sells footwear, apparel, and accessories globally. The company focuses on technical innovation and a premium brand image to attract runners and lifestyle consumers across the Americas, Europe, and Asia. It utilizes a mix of direct-to-consumer sales and selective wholesale partnerships to maintain high brand equity and control its pricing strategy.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached $4 billion, an increase of approximately 11% year over year. Net income for the period was nearly $527 million, reflecting a healthy net profit margin of 13%. The company has demonstrated a strong ability to scale its premium products while maintaining healthy bottom-line results.

As of its Sept. 2026 balance sheet, On carries a debt-to-equity ratio of roughly 0.4x. Its current ratio is approximately 3.1x, indicating a solid buffer of liquid assets to meet its upcoming financial obligations. Trailing free cash flow reached $478 million, providing the company with ample capital to reinvest in its global retail expansion.

e.l.f. Beauty must navigate a highly competitive landscape filled with both massive multinational corporations and nimble independent brands. The company relies heavily on a limited number of major retail partners, meaning the loss of shelf space at Target or Walmart could materially impact its results. Furthermore, its dependence on third-party manufacturers in China exposes the business to potential supply chain disruptions, shifts in international trade policy, or new tariffs. The integration of recent acquisitions also presents a risk if expected synergies fail to materialize.

On Holding faces intense competition from established global footwear players with significantly larger marketing budgets and distribution networks. While the brand is currently in high demand, it must continually innovate to keep pace with evolving fashion trends and athletic performance requirements. Managing inventory levels across diverse global regions is another constant challenge that could impact profitability if demand shifts unexpectedly. Additionally, as a premium brand, its sales remain sensitive to broader economic cycles that might limit discretionary spending on luxury sportswear.

On appears more attractive from a valuation perspective, as it trades at a lower multiple of both its future earnings estimates and its sales over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

These brands are both performing at a high level in a choppy consumer spending environment. I would buy e.l.f. Beauty, mainly because it is seeing accelerating growth and improving margins. I like On, too, which trades at a lower P/E multiple, but e.l.f. is growing revenue significantly faster through a challenging stretch for consumer brands. I think e.l.f. is building a powerful "house of brands" portfolio that is resonating at home and abroad.

E.l.f. posted a 51% year-over-year increase in net sales last quarter, marking the 31st straight quarter of 20% or better growth. Rhode seems to be getting more popular with each new release, while the company's Naturium brand is one of the fastest-growing in the skincare space.

E.l.f. has become the top cosmetics brand on Amazon, and management says it's also the most searched brand on the platform. People are seeking out the brand, which means the company doesn't have to spend as much on marketing to acquire new customers. This supports profitable expansion.

On is an attractive buy right now, too, as it continues to post double-digit growth while the stock trades at a modest forward P/E of about 18 -- a slight discount to the S&P 500's 19x forward P/E. But I prefer investing in reasonably priced growth stocks of companies that are growing revenue at higher rates. At the rate e.l.f. continues to grow with ample room to expand internationally, I believe the stock has a favorable chance of outperforming in 2027.

#2964. SpaceX’s December Lock-Up Frees Another 17% of Its Shares. Here’s What October’s Unlock Did to the Stock.

SpaceX's public float continues to balloon.

By Ryan Vanzo – Nov 18, 2026 at 9:35PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Space Exploration Technologies ›

Space Exploration Technologies' (SPCX +1.27%) initial public offering this summer was unique in several ways.

It was one of the biggest IPOs in history. It was also one of the largest public debuts ever for an unprofitable business. Finally, the company went public with less than 5% of its shares available for public purchase -- far below the industry average.

There are some benefits to going public with only a small fraction of your total share count available for trade. First, it limits initial selling pressure, as most of the shares are locked until future milestones are met. Second, it can stack the odds in favor of a successful debut, as buying pressure can easily overwhelm selling pressure.

There are downsides to SpaceX's approach, however. Over time, more SpaceX shares become available for trading, adding potential selling pressure. Some events call for large share unlocks, potentially flooding the market with tradable shares over a short period.

Several analysts were worried about a potential overhang from major share unlock events. Morningstar (MORN -0.65%) analysts, for example, were concerned not only with SpaceX's initial IPO valuation, but also the company's unusual staggered lockup schedule.

"We think long-term investors eager to participate in SpaceX's future endeavors and potential success will have opportunities to do so with more margin of safety than the initial offering is likely to provide," the firm concluded. SpaceX's lockup schedule, one Morningstar analyst warned, could create "regular bouts of selling pressure."

Next month, one of SpaceX's biggest share-unlock events will take place, freeing up roughly 17% of its total locked shares for sale. Will this create selling pressure as some experts predict?

The best way to answer this question is by analyzing SpaceX's October share price action. That month, several unlock events sent the company's public float soaring. The impact is critical for SpaceX investors to understand.

SpaceX experienced three share unlock events in October.

The first was on Oct. 9 when 328.4 million shares became eligible for trading. That represented around 7% of the shares covered by the lockup schedule, excluding founder shares, mostly owned by Elon Musk, which won't be eligible for sale until next summer. Shares barely responded to this even, moving higher by 1% on the day.

The next event on Oct. 22 triggered the release of 1.3 billion shares. That's around 28% of the locked-up pool. That trading session was rough, with SpaceX stock falling roughly 9%.

The final October unlock event began Oct. 24 with the release of 328.4 million shares valued at nearly $50 billion. Shares fell by just 0.4% the next day of trading.

First, major unlock events can affect a company's near-term stock price. SpaceX's smaller unlocks had little immediate effect. But its larger unlock events seemingly sent shares plunging. Despite strong third-quarter earnings and 11 successful rocket launches, shares fell roughly 9% in October.

Second, share unlock events can create buying opportunities. SpaceX's intrinsic value as a business does not change as more shares are made available for trade. The share price action is simply a reflection of short-term supply and demand dynamics. If you like SpaceX as a company but have been waiting for a better entry point, the next share unlock event on Dec. 9 could be your chance to jump in.

With a market cap of nearly $2 trillion, I'm still on the sidelines when it comes to SpaceX stock. As I explained recently, "SpaceX's current valuation is tough to justify based on launch services and Starlink potential alone. The company must execute on its AI roadmap to justify a valuation above $1 trillion."

SpaceX's AI roadmap, however, will be difficult to fully achieve. It relies on several high-risk, high-reward efforts, including the successful commercialization of Starship -- which would become the largest rocket to ever reach orbit -- and the successful deployments of orbital data centers, which some experts believe will never be economical.

December's unlock event could create additional selling pressure, providing investors with a more attractive entry point. But it still might not be enough for me to jump in.

#2959. La-Z-Boy: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Its U.S.-made upholstery and consolidated distribution hubs give investors a supply chain built to weather trade policy swings.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 18, 2026 at 10:09PM EST

On its fiscal 2027 second-quarter earnings call, held on Nov. 18, 2026, La-Z-Boy (LZB +3.63%) reported a 4% increase in consolidated delivered sales, excluding the impact of its wholesale casegoods divestiture. The company relies on retail store expansion and supply chain consolidation to strengthen its competitive position in a choppy market. Three key takeaways from management's comments stand out.

La-Z-Boy added three company-owned stores during the second quarter. Corporate ownership now represents 62% of its 380-store network. Owning more retail locations gives the company direct control over pricing and customer experience while capturing higher gross margins.

Management is consolidating Joybird production into its U.S. manufacturing network and reducing regional distribution centers from 15 facilities to three central hubs. Producing products domestically shields the business from ocean freight disruptions and shifting tariff policies.

The company returned $32 million to shareholders through dividends and share repurchases in the second quarter. Management maintains a clear framework to reinvest half of operating cash flow into the business while returning the rest to investors.

Choppy market conditions test furniture demand, but domestic manufacturing and network expansion preserve market share. Management monitors holiday traffic while maintaining pricing discipline.

Zero external debt provides financial flexibility to complete distribution upgrades while returning steady capital to shareholders, positioning the company for long-term growth.
