#2955. Fed Chair Kevin Warsh Vowed to Hit the Fed's 2% Inflation Target After October's Rate Hold, Even as JPMorgan Warns Democratic Control of the Senate Could Make Him a One-Term Chair. Here's Why That Political Risk Matters for Markets.

Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh is focusing on process, but that may not matter if he ignores this part of his job.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 18, 2026 at 10:15PM EST

10 stocks we like better than JPMorgan Chase ›

JPMorgan Chase (JPM -1.77%) recently published a report that went well beyond Wall Street analysis. In it, chief U.S. economist Michael Feroli warned that current Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh needs to broaden his focus, or all the changes he is making could prove for naught. It isn't an idle warning, and investors need to understand why politics matters for a position intended to be apolitical. The key to the story is that Kevin Warsh risks serving a one-term tenure as chair if he doesn't engage more with Congress. Here's why it matters.

From a big-picture perspective, the Fed's job is to ensure stable prices and full employment. That dual mandate can be difficult to manage because elevated inflation often accompanies a strong economy. It is a delicate balancing act meant to occur outside the political process. This is why the Fed is self-funded, board members serve long terms, and they can be removed only for cause. This setup is meant to curb the short-term thinking that often accompanies the political process, in which elections are held every two years.

Image source: Official Federal Reserve Photo.

That's not to suggest that the Fed can completely ignore politicians. The president appoints key members of the Fed to their positions, with the Senate's approval. The Fed also has to make regular updates to Congress. So while the job of Federal Reserve Chair is meant to be outside of the political process, it is still entwined with politics. That's the core of the warning that JPMorgan Chase's Michael Feroli is making.

According to Feroli, current Fed Chair Kevin Warsh is making a mistake by not engaging more with Congress. And given the shifting political winds in Washington, he could be replaced when his term ends in 2030 if he doesn't change what he is doing in this area. This warning is likely tied to the fact that Warsh is making major changes in other areas of his job.

Even as the Fed has made strong statements about returning inflation to its 2% target, Kevin Warsh has been vocal about reducing the amount of guidance it provides to the market. Wall Street got used to receiving material guidance after the dot-com crash and the Great Recession. Effectively, the Fed was looking to ease investor concerns during deep bear markets and economic downturns. However, the guidance didn't end after those difficult periods.

Warsh believes this has left the market reliant on the Fed rather than operating as it should, setting prices in real time based on economic conditions. As such, he's started to limit the amount of information the Fed shares. Wall Street isn't pleased, particularly with inflation elevated and rates on the rise. The bond market has been particularly volatile during this transition. For those who have been around long enough, however, this is just a return to more normal times, even though it is not an easy transition.

That is where Warsh's interactions with Congress come in. If he is only a one-term Fed chair, the changes could quickly be rolled back by the next chairman. If he takes the time to better explain what he is doing and why, he would increase the odds of being appointed to a second term. That would help the changes he has made stick, which, in turn, could give the Fed more leeway to address its dual mandate over the long term.

Warsh is focused on fixing "the process," which is laudable. There is a moral hazard in Wall Street expecting the Fed to provide excessive information and, in some quarters, to serve as a backstop for the market. But fixing the process isn't happening in a vacuum, and Warsh would increase the odds of success if he took the time to more fully, and perhaps personally, explain to Congress what he is doing and why.

While that sounds political, and it is to some extent, it may be more of a social issue. If Warsh doesn't build trust and respect, he risks reducing the support he'll receive when his term ends. The risk for investors is that the Fed's approach could swing between extremes, which isn't likely to be good for anybody.

#2950. 4 Things the Headlines Missed in Nvidia's First Post-Jensen Earnings Report

Nvidia easily beat third-quarter expectations, yet the real story is hiding in the fine print. Interim CEO Colette Kress just dialed back the sales swagger and put customer financing on a tighter leash.

By Anders Bylund – Nov 18, 2026 at 10:47PM EST

The leather jacket is gone, and the spreadsheets are officially in charge.

Reporting its first quarterly results just days after former CEO Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation, Nvidia (NVDA -2.90%) proved that its AI dominance doesn't depend on one charismatic ringmaster. In Q3 of fiscal year 2027, Nvidia's revenue soared 103% year over year to $115.8 billion, the new Vera Rubin architecture racked up nearly $20 billion in sales in its debut quarter, and interim CEO Colette Kress even raised the company's fiscal 2028 revenue growth target from 70% to 75%.

But beneath those breathtaking headline numbers, interim CEO Colette Kress delivered the report with the measured calm you expect from a 13-year veteran CFO. Kress toned down some of her predecessor's boldest growth framing while tightening the guardrails around how Nvidia deploys its capital.

It's a serious top-level strategy shift, brought on by an unexpected scandal. You can see most of it in the earnings release and prepared CFO comments without even digging into the earnings call.

And I think I like Kress's attitude. For the first time in forever, Nvidia's business plan makes sense to me.

Back in the second quarter, Nvidia couldn't resist hyping up its $105 billion lease guarantee for OpenAI's massive Ohio AI data center campus, telling investors that each generation of chips installed there could generate $150 billion to $200 billion in Nvidia revenue.

In her debut quarter at the helm, Kress deleted that $200 billion sales pitch from the report. Instead, she offered a step-by-step breakdown of how Nvidia's credit guarantee will take effect across nine separate buildings starting in fiscal year 2029. She also reassured investors by noting that Nvidia hasn't yet paid out any of its financing guarantees. The expected growth is still record-breaking, but on a scale from 1 to 10, Kress dialed back the marketing swagger from 11 to 6.

A longtime CFO could be expected to starve Nvidia's growth engine in order to hoard cash. The supply chain numbers say that Nvidia will have plenty of growth fuel under new management.

Kress lifted Nvidia's multi-year supply and memory chip commitments from $279 billion in Q2 to $390 billion in Q3. The boost springs from incredible demand for the Vera Rubin AI accelerator.

Where did she tighten things up? That would be in the customer financing area. Reported additional commitments related to cash-burning "neocloud" start-ups fell from $56 billion to $32 billion. A new warning in the reporting boilerplate said that those commitments can be "reduced or eliminated" if the neoclouds fail to line up their own financing. And on the call, Kress noted that the underlying deal terms haven't changed; Nvidia will simply be "disciplined and selective" about these contracts. Non-investment-grade customers had better have their capital and end buyers lined up before Nvidia commits to shipping them GPUs from a scarce supply.

To gauge Nvidia's financial health, Wall Street spends hours and hours analyzing the capital spending budgets of Microsoft, Alphabet, and Meta Platforms. Hyperscalers are no longer the fastest-growing part of the business, though. And they may soon become the smaller slice, too.

Hyperscale revenue climbed 71% year over year to $60.1 billion, but Nvidia's AI Clouds, Industrial, and Enterprise segment roughly tripled. Sales in this segment jumped 195% to $47.5 billion. The roster of non-tech-giant customers is broadening fast, including sovereign AI initiatives, enterprise quantitative trading clusters, and stand-alone Vera CPU sales.

With Nvidia currently ringing up nearly $10 billion in sales every week, that extra calendar week accounts for more than 60% of the sequential revenue jump between Q3 and Q4. The underlying demand for Nvidia's AI silicon is definitely strong. Still, investors should remember that the calendar is giving Q4 a temporary tailwind this year -- and next year's 13-week fourth quarter will face the same calendar quirk in reverse.

#2946. The S&P 500 Just Ended a Run of 155 Trading Days Above Its 200-Day Average. Here's What Followed 34 Similar Runs Since 1950.

The index just ended a 155-session run above that line. What came after the 34 earlier runs of 150-plus sessions?

By Daniel Sparks – Nov 18, 2026 at 11:04PM EST

10 stocks we like better than S&P 500 Index ›

The S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.23%) finished Tuesday, Nov. 17, at 7,340.53. That left it a few points under its 200-day moving average (the average of its past 200 daily closes, or around nine and a half months of trading), which was near 7,345.

It was the index's first close below that line since April 7. The next day, the S&P 500 began a run of 155 straight sessions above its average, posting a record close of 7,818.93 on Oct. 6 along the way.

Its worst day since that high was Nov. 5, when it dropped over 4% as chipmakers and other stocks linked to artificial intelligence (AI) sold off. As I write, the index is roughly 6% below its record. That still puts it about 7% above where it closed 2025.

With tech stocks leading the slide, a break like this may look like the market's longer-term trend turning down. Does it normally bring more trouble?

I went through the index's history since 1950 and found 34 earlier times a run of at least 150 sessions above the 200-day average ended. Three months later, the index was up in 26 of them.

My numbers come from Yahoo Finance's record of the index's daily closes since January 1950. These cover only price changes, not dividends. For every day, I averaged the 200 most recent closes, then counted how many sessions in a row the index finished above its average.

The longest run, at 628 sessions, ended in May 1956. Notably, four of the last six breaks happened this decade on Feb. 27, 2020, Jan. 21, 2022, March 10, 2025, and March 19 this year.

After the March break, the index dropped about 4% further in the next seven sessions, closed back above its average on April 8, and was around 14% higher by mid-June.

In half the 34 cases, the index closed back above its 200-day average within five trading days, and in 28 of them, it did so within 20 sessions (about four weeks). But the 1987 break was the big outlier -- the index took 161 sessions to get back above its average.

Across all 34 breaks, the index climbed over the next three months 76% of the time, with a median gain of around 4%. By comparison, a three-month stretch from any random trading day since 1950 finished with a gain in about two out of three cases (67%), with a median gain closer to 3%. In other words, the months after a break have arguably been a bit better than an ordinary stretch.

Still, the exceptions were rough. The worst came after April 4, 1962, when the index dropped about 17% over the next three months.

Another break came on Oct. 15, 1987, two sessions before the crash of Oct. 19, when the index dropped about 20% in one day. Three months after that break, it was still down roughly 15%.

The year after a break looks even more like an ordinary year. Of the 33 breaks that are at least a year old, the index ended the next 12 months up in 23, or around 70%, and the median gain was about 9%. In a normal 12-month period since 1950, it's climbed about 75% of the time, with a median gain of about 11%.

Of course, a break didn't always mean a quiet year. In 11 of the 33 cases, the index slid another 10% or more below its break-day close at some point within 12 months. That set includes August 2007, when the index was down around 13% a year later.

But it also includes February 2020 and March 2025. Both times, the index fell further within weeks, by around 25% through March 23, 2020, and about 11% through April 8, 2025. And both times, it ended the next 12 months higher, up about 28% and 21%, respectively.

So, should a long-term holder of a fund tracking the S&P 500 sell after a break like this? I don't think so.

In most of these cases, selling would've meant missing gains in the months that followed.

And on the day of each break, the ones that turned into deeper drops didn't look very different from the rest. At the median, the index stood around 8% under its high of the previous year in those cases, against about 7% in the others.

I'd keep holding, though I think the index could easily fall more from here.

#2943. Top Wall Street analysts recommend these 3 dividend stocks for higher returns

Dividend-paying stocks can enhance portfolio returns by providing a steady income stream to investors and the potential for capital appreciation. Several investors seek exposure to dividend stocks in a volatile backdrop to ensure stable income.

In this regard, recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, backed by strong financials and cash flows to support consistent dividend payments.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Energy Transfer (ET) is a diversified midstream energy company with about 140,000 miles of pipelines and associated energy infrastructure in 44 states. The company made a quarterly cash distribution of 34 cents per common unit for the second quarter of 2026. At an annualized payment of $1.36 per common unit, ET offers a yield of 6.3%.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Jeremy Tonet reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer stock and slightly raised his price target to $25 from $24. The analyst noted that the company delivered a strong Q2 beat across all key metrics.

Tonet highlighted that Energy Transfer raised its 2026 adjusted EBITDA guidance to $18.8 billion-$19.1 billion from $18.2 billion-$18.6 billion, reflecting robust base results and cost optimization. The 5-star analyst added that the company "tightened" its capex to $5.6 billion–$5.9 billion and expects capex above $5 billion annually through 2029, given solid opportunities.

About the Hugh Brinson pipeline, Tonet noted Phase 1 (1.5 bcfd) reaching full capacity by September 1 and Phase II capacity (0.7 bcfd) going online by Q1 2027. The analyst also noted completion of a 14-mile Hugh Brinson lateral in Abilene, Texas, and final stages of talks for an additional 250 mmcfd of Oklahoma power plant demand.

"All in, we see ET well positioned to continue capitalizing on organic growth opportunities across the value chain," said Tonet.

Tonet ranks No. 922 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 9%. See Energy Transfer Financials on TipRanks.

This week's second dividend pick is independent oil and natural gas company Permian Resources (PR). The company declared a base dividend of $0.16 per share for the third quarter, payable on September 30, 2026. At an annualized dividend of $0.64 per share, PR offers a yield of about 2.7%.

Recently, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Permian Resources stock and increased his price target to $27 from $22, citing an improved outlook on oil production growth and operational efficiencies.

The 5-star analyst noted that while PR stock has outperformed its peers so far in 2026, he sees further upside. Mehta is confident about the company's operational improvements and ability to deliver incremental free cash flow per share through the strategic ground game program. He added that the program has completed about $1.05 billion in bolt-on deals this year (as of August 5).

"We see the continued success of PR's ground game as constructive toward future inventory replenishment and increasing working interest over time," said Mehta.

The analyst also emphasized Permian Resources' solid track record of generating growth in free cash flow (FCF) per share. Mehta expects the company's free cash flow per share to grow at a 20% CAGR (compound annual growth rate) from 2025–2028.

Mehta ranks No. 401 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.8%. See Permian Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

Moving on to Sempra Energy (SRE), a utility holding company. Earlier this month, the company declared a quarterly dividend of $0.6575 per share, payable on October 15. At an annualized dividend of $2.63 per share, SRE stock offers a yield of about 3.1%.

Recently, Jefferies analyst Julien Dumoulin-Smith upgraded SRE stock to Buy from Hold but slightly lowered the price target to $97 from $101. The 5-star analyst noted that Sempra stock is trading at a price/earnings multiple that is 14% below its electric peers due to concerns about Texas transmission capital expenditure and the failure of California legislation.

Smith noted that the derating of weaker California peers PG&E (PCG) and Edison International (EIX) is weighing on the sum-of-the-parts (SOTP) valuation but is becoming less relevant given their materially higher fire risk compared to Sempra Energy.

Furthermore, Smith expects Sempra Energy's Texas capex plan to stay in place despite the opposition to transmission and data centers, though delays to 765-kV projects are likely. While a full recovery for SRE stock will need a clearer outcome from the Texas legislative session, Smith believes that the stock may already be an early opportunity for patient investors.

"We are early on the upgrade but see limited downside at current levels as the market is pricing in little transmission upside," said Smith.

Smith ranks No. 945 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.2%. See Sempra ETF Exposure on TipRanks.

#2941. Magnite vs. The Trade Desk: Which Ad Tech Stock Is a Better Buy for 2027?

Two ad-tech leaders, two very different business models, and a 2027 pick that depends on which side of the programmatic ad transaction appears stronger right now.

By Robert Izquierdo – Updated Nov 18, 2026 at 11:36PM EST

Automated, or programmatic, advertising is reshaping the media landscape by providing more transparency and efficiency for both ad buyers and sellers. Choosing between Magnite (MGNI -2.46%) and The Trade Desk (TTD +3.95%) requires deciding which side of the transaction offers the most potential.

Magnite focuses on helping media owners maximize the value of their digital inventory, while The Trade Desk empowers agencies and brands to buy that inventory effectively. Both companies are at the center of the shift toward programmatic advertising, making them essential players in the evolving digital ecosystem.

Magnite operates in the digital advertising market, with a strong presence among online publishers and content providers requiring automated tools to sell inventory. As a supply-side platform, the company provides the technology that media owners use to manage their ad space. Two major buyers indirectly contributed nearly 44% of its revenue in 2025. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue reached $766.7 million, representing growth of 9.1% compared to the prior year. The company reported net income of $180.8 million during this period. The performance reflects a steady expansion in its ability to monetize video and connected TV inventory across global markets.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the debt-to-equity ratio was 0.7x. This ratio compares total debt to shareholder equity to show how much leverage a company uses. The current ratio, which measures if a company can cover its short-term obligations with its current assets, was approximately 1.0x. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow was $165.6 million. Note that stock-based compensation (SBC) represented 32.5% of operating cash flow, which inflates reported cash generation since SBC is a non-cash expense added back in the cash flow statement.

The Trade Desk provides a self-service, cloud-based platform that allows advertising buyers to manage data-driven campaigns across multiple devices. The company primarily serves advertising agencies and major brands that need to optimize their spending in real time. In 2025, two unnamed holding companies each represented more than 10% of the company's gross billings. Customer concentration like this adds a layer of risk to the business.

In the 12 months ended Sept. 30, 2026, revenue was $3.0 billion, which was a 7% increase over the previous 12-month period. Net income for that same time frame reached $329.4 million. This steady top-line growth is supported by its broad network of hundreds of integrated ad exchanges and data vendors.

As of its December 2025 balance sheet, the company maintained a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.2x. The current ratio stood at 1.6x, indicating a solid buffer of liquid assets to handle short-term liabilities. In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, free cash flow reached $795.7 million. Note that stock-based compensation represented 49.4% of operating cash flow.

The Trade Desk trades at a significantly lower multiple of its sales, while Magnite offers a higher price relative to its future earnings estimates.

Valuation metrics include sourcing from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from other data providers.

Magnite and The Trade Desk represent opposite sides of the digital advertising coin. I believe the buy side, represented by The Trade Desk, is the stronger part of the ecosystem, as demonstrated by the rise of businesses such as Amazon. However, between these two ad tech giants, I recommend investing in Magnite right now.

The Trade Desk has been struggling as competition has intensified. Amazon and other new competitors battling for ad buying dollars, including many streaming services, have grown in recent years. Also, the artificial intelligence boom led to greater adoption of AI tools that appear to be eating into The Trade Desk's offerings.

The company exited 2025 with 18% year-over-year revenue growth to $2.9 billion, yet sales expansion has been elusive in 2026. In its latest quarterly earnings report, for the third quarter of 2026, The Trade Desk's sales fell 0.5% year over year to $736 million.

Magnite's business is headed in the opposite direction, as sales growth has accelerated. In Q1 of 2026, revenue rose 6% year over year. That jumped to 11% in Q2, then 14% in Q3. While the advent of new businesses into digital advertising has hurt The Trade Desk, this has helped Magnite, with streaming companies using its platform to monetize their content.

#2936. Should You Buy Take-Two Interactive After the GTA VI Release?

The long-awaited GTA VI video game is expected to deliver a "historic year" for Take-Two Interactive.

By Dave Kovaleski – Nov 19, 2026 at 12:01AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Take-Two Interactive Software ›

Gamers have been waiting more than a decade -- 13 years, to be exact -- for the latest edition of the popular Grand Theft Auto (GTA) video game franchise, and it's finally here. It's not just Christmas morning for GTA fans; it is like Christmas after getting coal in your stocking 13 years in a row.

GTA VI, produced by Take-Two Interactive (TTWO +6.22%), comes out on Nov. 19 at midnight in the Pacific time zone, and Take-Two's stock surged in advance of the release. That’s late Wednesday evening or early Thursday morning, depending on how you look at it. On Wednesday, Nov. 18, Take-Two stock jumped nearly 7% to around $220 per share, but it is still down about 15% year-to-date.

Is the release of GTA VI the catalyst that Take-Two stock needs to take off?

According to industry reports, GTA VI has received some 17.1 million pre-orders, with net revenue from the sales at $1.65 billion. This is reportedly the largest pre-order total for a video game ever.

On Take-Two's recent earnings call, CEO Strauss Zelnick said pre-orders "are tracking far in excess of our initial expectations."

For fiscal 2027, Take-Two anticipates net bookings of $8.0 billion to $8.2 billion -- that is revenue from games sold and in-game purchases. This would be up some 21% at the midpoint from the $6.7 billion in net bookings in fiscal 2026. The difference is largely due to the boost from GTA VI.

For perspective, GTA V, the last version (which came out in 2013), has sold 235 million copies, making it the second-best-selling video game of all time, behind Minecraft. The entire Grand Theft Auto franchise has sold about 480 million copies.

GTA is not Take-Two's only game franchise. It also owns the NBA 2K and Red Dead Redemption franchises, along with mobile games through Zynga, including Words with Friends, but GTA is its primary revenue driver.

For fiscal 2027, Take-Two raised its revenue guidance to $7.98 billion to $8.18 billion, up from $7.9 billion to $8.1 billion, and boosted its earnings per share range to $0.57 to $0.75, up from $0.55 to $0.75. It also expects to generate more than $1 billion in net cash, which will help it grow.

Zelnick said it will be a "historic year for our company," as it looks to "explore emerging business opportunities, expand our geographic footprint, and pursue accretive M&A."

Take-Two stock has dropped about 15% year-to-date as net bookings fell 18% to $1.6 billion in its Q2 while revenue plunged 15% year over year to $1.50 billion. The firm had a net loss of $54 million, or $0.29 per share last quarter.

But for Q3, it expects to double its net bookings to $3.35 billion to $3.5 billion, due mostly to GTA VI sales, while net income is anticipated to soar to $250 million to $276 million, or $1.30 to $1.44 per share.

Some 94% of Wall Street analysts rate Take-Two stock as a buy, with a median price target of $290 per share. That would suggest 32% upside.

With a forward price-to-earnings ratio of 28, Take-Two stock is not cheap, but it is not overvalued either, relative to its growth expectations. The GTA VI effect will have a multi-year impact on sales, as GTA V has shown, with analysts anticipating $9 billion in revenue in fiscal 2028, as a consensus.

There may be a sell-the-news dip, as some of the recent gains have already been priced in, given strong pre-order results and widespread anticipation. Since it dipped to $183 per share on Oct. 30, Take-Two stock has jumped about 20% to $220 per share. So perhaps it's best to allow the price to settle a bit. But in the longer term, Take-Two stock looks like a solid buy.

#2932. Why Anthropic Could Be a $5 Trillion Company By 2030

Investors have never seen a company like Anthropic before.

By Jeremy Bowman – Nov 19, 2026 at 12:19AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Anthropic, PBC ›

Anthropic (Nasdaq: ANT) is about to make stock market history.

The AI lab will go public on Thursday at a valuation of $1.5 trillion after pricing its IPO at $83 a share. While that is slightly below the $1.75 trillion that Space Exploration Technologies was valued at in its debut, Anthropic will raise more money than SpaceX, bringing in $107.3 billion, including a $26 private placement from some big tech companies.

Anthropic is just five years old, born from former OpenAI employees, and has experienced meteoric growth, with revenue up 1,200% through the first three quarters of the year. Its revenue run rate reached $68 billion at the end of the third quarter. No company in history has come close to that mark just five years since its inception.

Anthropic faces a lot of risks as it goes public. The company has approximately $900 billion in future purchase commitments for chips, AI compute, and lease obligations. It faces competition from other frontier AI labs like OpenAI as well as open-source and open-weight models, which could eat into its market share. Additionally, CEO Dario Amodei has highlighted the risks of AI and called for a slowdown in development to ensure adequate guardrails are in place.

Despite those risks, the company also has a lot of upside potential, and reaching a $5 trillion market cap is achievable. Here's why.

Anthropic might have more growth potential than any other company in history. We've already seen this anecdotally. Software stocks plunged at the beginning of the year when Anthropic introduced a series of plug-in tools for Claude Cowork, competing with specialized software for job functions like legal, finance, and sales.

The disruptive potential for Anthropic is virtually limitless, considering potential applications that extend to drug discovery, robotics, chip design, and beyond.

That's why it doesn't seem hyperbolic for the company to claim a market opportunity ranging from $22.5 trillion to $40 trillion. In its prospectus, Anthropic said that the International Data Corporation (IDC) estimates that AI will produce a $22.5 trillion impact by 2031, and Anthropic believes it could be even greater. Management also said that work performed by knowledge workers represented more than $40 trillion in economic value in 2025.

By both increasing knowledge worker productivity and shifting it to Claude, Anthropic certainly has a multi-trillion-dollar opportunity in front of it, which could support the stock tripling through 2030.

Anthropic has delivered incredible growth over the last year as its new frontier models have grabbed market share from OpenAI and driven a surge in Claude adoption.

In the third quarter, revenue jumped 1,127% year-over-year to $17 billion, rising 47% on a sequential basis. Over a year, 47% sequential growth would translate into 366% revenue growth on an annual basis. While we don't know what Anthropic's growth rate will be going forward, it clearly has the potential to deliver breakout growth if it releases new products and expands its addressable market.

Not only is Anthropic growing year-over-year revenue by more than 1,000%, but the company is now profitable on an adjusted basis with an adjusted operating profit of $4.1 billion through the first three quarters, and $2.5 billion in the third quarter. Anthropic's business is scaling as it grows, and its margins are expanding.

Based on the company's run rate revenue of $68 billion and the IPO valuation of $1.5 trillion, the stock is valued at 22 times sales, which looks like a bargain considering its growth rate and profitability.

It's not unusual for software stocks to trade at around 20 times sales, and the ones that do don't have the explosive growth or disruptive potential that Anthropic does, and some aren't even profitable on an adjusted basis.

With a massive addressable market and soaring growth rate, Anthropic could become a $5 trillion company sooner than investors expect, especially if it maintains a strong growth rate. Don't be fooled by the dialed-down IPO valuation.

#2930. GRAIL: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Promotional pricing trades near-term margin for adoption, and that bet only pays off if per-test costs fall as volumes scale.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 19, 2026 at 12:53AM EST

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 4, 2026, GRAIL (GRAL -3.57%) highlighted a positive Food and Drug Administration (FDA) advisory committee vote for its Galleri multi-cancer early detection (MCED) test. FDA premarket approval (PMA), the agency's review process for high-risk medical devices, would create a distinct market class for Galleri.

It'd establish the test as the only approved screening tool that detects dozens of cancer types from one blood draw. Three things management said on the earnings call stand out for long-term investors.

An expert panel backed Galleri through the PMA review pathway in September. That vote positions the test to become the first FDA-approved multi-cancer screening tool. Management expects a final decision early in the first quarter of 2027, which would allow GRAIL to differentiate its product from unapproved competitors.

GRAIL introduced a promotional price in September to lower cost barriers in the self-pay market. That promotion pushed average selling price down to roughly $660 per test. Management is deliberately accepting lower gross margins today to expand provider adoption, build repeat testing habits, and prepare for insurance coverage.

Higher lab volumes should lower cost of goods sold (COGS) by about $100 per unit. That cost reduction helps offset a legacy royalty payment to Illumina that resumes in late December 2026. Management targets long-term adjusted gross margins of 50% to 60% as unit processing costs drop toward $230 at scale.

Commercial momentum's building across provider channels and electronic health record integrations. GRAIL's leaning on its clinical data set to secure coverage with private insurers and government payers.

A cash balance of $788.7 million provides financial runway into 2029 while GRAIL expands marketing and sales coverage. Management's aligning capital deployment to build repeat testing demand once insurance reimbursement opens.

#2927. Moderna's Cancer Vaccine Worked -- and the Stock Plunged Anyway. Here's What Investors Are Missing.

Moderna's melanoma vaccine plus Keytruda cut the risk of recurrence or death by 42% in Phase 3, yet the stock sank. Here's why.

By Rick Orford – Nov 19, 2026 at 1:00AM EST

Moderna (MRNA -4.84%) delivered promising results for its experimental cancer vaccine on Oct. 22, yet its stock plunged anyway. But looking past the headlines, there's more to the story than the trial results suggest, and investors may be overlooking the real opportunity.

At first glance, Moderna delivered the kind of clinical milestone that investors had been waiting for: positive Phase 3 results for its personalized melanoma vaccine, intismeran autogene, developed in partnership with Merck. But despite the solid results, Moderna shares fell more than 25% during the Oct. 22 session.

The sell-off followed an unexpected leak of the trial results ahead of their scheduled presentation at the European Society for Medical Oncology (ESMO) Congress. In the trial, the vaccine, when administered alongside Merck's Keytruda, reduced the risk of cancer recurrence or death by 42% compared with Keytruda alone.

On paper, these were encouraging results, but Moderna stock had already rallied following an earlier Phase 2 study. Indeed, this appears to be the case of the market wanting something even better.

For example, some analysts expected the Phase 3 hazard ratio to come in below 0.60, which would mean the vaccine reduces the risk of recurrence or death by more than 40%. Yet the actual result came in at 0.58, which technically cleared that threshold, but only narrowly.

In the end, given the high expectations, investors viewed the results as a disappointment. But there's more here than the headline numbers suggest.

Several analysts softened their initial views after the full presentation, recognizing that the vaccine delivered a meaningful improvement over Keytruda alone. The vaccine requires a series of personalized immunizations, which reduced the risk of recurrence or death by 58% based on outcomes six months or more after the initial treatment.

On top of that, the safety profile was notable. In fact, serious treatment-related adverse events occurred in around 10% of patients receiving the Moderna-Merck combination, compared with 30% in the separate NADINA trial of Opdivo and Yervoy.

That means Moderna's approach can potentially deliver additional clinical benefits without necessarily requiring patients to tolerate more aggressive immunotherapy combinations. As such, intismeran could still become an attractive option for patients less likely to tolerate dual checkpoint blockade, a cancer treatment that uses two different immunotherapy drugs.

Perhaps more importantly, though, Moderna's opportunity could be much bigger than melanoma.

Intismeran uses personalized mRNA technology to train a patient's immune system to recognize specific mutations associated with their cancer. Unlike conventional vaccines designed to prevent infections, this approach is intended to help the immune system identify and attack cancer cells that may otherwise evade detection.

It's also where the broader opportunity becomes something more: If the technology proves effective across different cancer types, Moderna could develop an entirely new category of personalized cancer treatments.

In fact, Moderna and Merck are already assessing intismeran's potential for other types of cancer, including renal cell carcinoma, bladder cancer, and non-small cell lung cancer. Plus, Moderna presented preliminary data from a study evaluating intismeran alongside chemotherapy in pancreatic cancer, with 55% of patients remaining relapse-free after 17 months of follow-up.

Despite these developments, intismeran still requires FDA approval, and overall survival data remain preliminary. On top of that, its effectiveness in other cancer types has yet to be proven.

Finally, today's valuation is also a concern. Right now, Moderna stock trades at a price-to-sales (P/S) ratio of about 35 times, so investors continue to have high expectations around future growth. Meanwhile, Merck trades at a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio of about 16.

Ultimately, at today's valuation, the market may be focusing too much on one trial result and not on Moderna's longer-term business.

Overall, Moderna's cancer vaccine may not have met Wall Street analysts' expectations, but it's still a meaningful clinical achievement. Looking ahead, investors need to be patient as the vaccine moves toward commercialization, but dismissing Moderna based on one trial result could mean overlooking a much bigger opportunity.

#2925. Top Wall Street analysts find these 3 stocks attractive as long-term investments

Investors have been grappling with stock market volatility, weighing elevated oil prices, persistent geopolitical tensions, the Federal Reserve's rate hike and a debate about AI safety. For all that, it helps to look beyond short-term noise and focus on stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors gain clarity, as these experts conduct in-depth research to pick stocks with attractive growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Enterprise software and cloud infrastructure company Oracle (ORCL) recently announced better-than-expected results for the first quarter of fiscal 2027. The quarter benefited from solid growth in cloud infrastructure revenue.

After hosting a series of investor meetings with Ken Bond, Oracle head of investor relations following Q1 earnings, Guggenheim analyst John DiFucci reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $400. Oracle remains a Guggenheim Best Idea, he said.

"We continue to believe ORCL is a decade stock, driven by the massive (and profitable) opportunity ahead for AI training and inferencing," said DiFucci. He also cited the traditional Public Cloud business and additional AI-enabled cloud database and cloud applications for his bullish stance. DiFucci expects all the factors to drive revenue and profit acceleration over the next few years.

While Oracle delivered 850 MW of data center capacity in Q1 FY27, Bond cautioned that Infrastructure as a Service revenue in any quarter depends more on the timing of when that capacity comes online, than on the headline MW number. DiFucci expects IaaS growth to stay strong, with full-year growth expected above Q1 FY27's 120% constant currency rate. Management expects total revenue growth to accelerate sequentially throughout fiscal 2027, as the faster-growing infrastructure business makes up a larger share of the mix.

DiFucci addressed concerns regarding customer concentration, saying OpenAI's share of total remaining performance obligations has dropped from 80% last June to about 50% now, and is expected to keep falling as new contracts are signed with new and existing customers.

DiFucci ranks No. 263 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 62% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17.5%. See Oracle Insider Trading Activity on TipRanks.

Space systems and launch services provider Rocket Lab (RKLB) was recently initiated as a buy with an $80 price target by Raymond James analyst Brian Gesuale.

"RKLB has assembled launch, spacecraft, components, payloads and optical communications, with Neutron and Iridium completing medium-lift and applications/spectrum in 2027," said Gesuale.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that with the completion of the Iridium acquisition, Rocket Lab will become the only vertically integrated space company with positive free cash flow (FCF). The deal will add a deployed constellation valued at more than $3 billion, globally coordinated L-band spectrum and about $500 million in earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) and $300 million in free cash flow (FCF).

Gesuale highlighted RKLB's strong revenue and backlog growth and progress toward positive EBITDA and FCF in 2027/2028. Rocket Lab is moving from a heavy investment phase into monetization, with estimated revenue growth of 59% in 2026, the analyst said, noting that RKLB's backlog has more than doubled to almost $2.4 billion from $1.1 billion in Q3 2025.

Given strong growth projections, the analyst expects RKLB's gross margins to expand from about a 35% trough in 2027 to about 47% by 2030, while R&D spending is estimated to decline from ~35% of sales in 2026 to about 10% in 2028, creating substantial operating leverage as the platform matures.

Gesuale ranks No. 369 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of about 17%. See Rocket Lab Statistics on TipRanks.

J.P. Morgan analyst Doug Anmuth recently upgraded Meta Platforms (META) to buy from hold and raised his price target to $820 from $640. Despite the year-to-date underperformance of META, the 5-star analyst still sees significant upside, as Meta is in the early stages of launching frontier models and AI-powered offerings beyond advertising, particularly the Muse AI agent and Meta Model API access.

"Frontier models are at the core of Meta's product and monetization pipeline over a multi-year period, and its path toward superintelligence," said Anmuth.

In the summer of 2025, Meta set a goal to rebuild its Meta Superintelligence Labs team and deliver frontier AI models within a year, the analyst said. The team appears to have met its goal, rapidly advancing from the rollout of Muse Spark 1.1 in July to Muse Spark 1.3, which is competitive with Claude and GPT models, Anmuth said.

Meanwhile, Meta's Watermelon product will expand opportunities in consumer products, business intelligence, and Family of Apps engagement, the analyst said. The product will enhance internal operations and overall efficiency, said Anmuth, who's confident of Meta's ability to bring consumer-driven AI products to its extensive user base of 4 billion and views its distribution network as a competitive advantage.

While Meta has focused on frontier models and AI product development, Anmuth also sees solid opportunity in its AI infrastructure capacity, which he believes will support future compute needs.

Anmuth ranks No. 832 among more than 12,490 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of about 9.40%. See Meta Platforms Financials on TipRanks.

10 stocks we like better than Sigma Lithium ›

#2919. Planet Labs Teams Up With Stoke Space -- Just In Case SpaceX Really Retires the Falcon 9

If SpaceX retires the Falcon 9, Planet Labs will be ready with a replacement ride to space.

By Rich Smith – Nov 19, 2026 at 1:30AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Planet Labs PBC ›

Planet Labs owns the world's biggest constellation of Earth observation satellites -- approximately 200 satellites in orbit, comprising the Dove and SkySat constellations that capture over 25 terabytes of imagery each day. These satellites only last about three to five years in orbit, however, before they fall out of the sky and must be replaced. This means that, even without growing its constellation size, Planet Labs needs to continually launch rockets with new satellites to refresh its fleet.

And here's the problem: Space Exploration Technologies (SPCX +1.27%), the company that launches most of these satellites on its Falcon 9 rockets, is planning to retire those vehicles.

I argued recently that once SpaceX realizes how big of a problem it's creating for other space companies by retiring its Falcon -- and how much money it could make if it keeps Falcon operating -- SpaceX might reconsider and postpone Falcon's retirement.

But Planet Labs is making contingency plans. The company announced it has signed a multi-launch agreement with private space start-up Stoke Space Technologies.

As Planet Labs explains, its contract calls for Stoke to carry Planet Labs' satellites atop 15-ton-payload Stoke Nova reusable rockets, which will begin launching from Cape Canaveral Space Launch Complex 14 (SLC-14) in 2027.

No price was specified in the contract announcement, so we don't know exactly how much Planet Labs will pay for these launches. It's also not 100% clear when Stoke will launch the first Planet satellites.

Stoke has announced an early 2027 target date for the 1.0 version of Nova, which it is calling Nova Pathfinder. The first launch will carry payloads from space-mining company AstroForge and space-cremation company Celestis, which may leave little room for Planet Labs’ satellites.

Additional Nova Pathfinder missions are planned throughout 2027 and 2028, however, before Stoke transitions to a larger model: Nova Block 2. And even before Block 2 arrives, the original recipe Nova Pathfinder rocket is expected to have a 3-ton payload to low earth orbit -- more than enough to replace Planet Labs' entire SuperDove constellation in one go.

Long story short, Payload appears to have found itself a workable solution to the problem of vanishing Falcon 9 rockets. If Falcon missions do end in 2028 as expected, and Stoke succeeds in getting Nova operational this year -- or even next year or the year after that -- it should arrive in time for Planet Labs to avoid any SpaceX-related complications to its business plan.

#2917. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Modifications to the Capital Plan Rule and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

#2911. Tempus AI: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Positive adjusted EBITDA signals the diagnostic platform has crossed a profitability threshold where test volume growth flows directly to earnings.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 19, 2026 at 1:35AM EST

10 stocks we like better than Tempus AI ›

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 2, 2026, Tempus AI (TEM +0.30%) reported total revenue of $411.5 million, up 23% year over year. The diagnostic and data platform generated $25.6 million in adjusted EBITDA, or earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization.

Rising test prices and multiyear biopharma licensing agreements provide predictable funding for future software development. Three things management said on the call stand out.

Data and applications revenue rose 33% year over year to $108 million, driven by long-term licensing of real-world patient records to biopharma partners, such as Pathos AI. Multiyear collaborations with major pharmaceutical companies secure predictable cash flows as clients train artificial intelligence models inside the company's platform.

Reimbursement expansion for clinical tests directly boosts diagnostic gross margins without requiring higher sales expenses. Tempus expects substantial gains in average selling prices (ASPs) as its XT solid tumor profiling test transitions to approved regulatory pricing from the Food and Drug Administration (FDA).

Gross profit growth outpaced operational investments during the quarter, helping the business reach positive operating profit targets. Generating positive adjusted EBITDA shows that expanding diagnostic test volumes drop directly down to the bottom line.

Compounding technology investments and clinical datasets reinforce competitive positioning across oncology markets. Strategic execution prioritizes gross profit reinvestment to maintain diagnostic leadership.

Sustained revenue expansion hinges on long-term execution across clinical sequencing and data licensing.

#2905. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for steady income

Stock markets remain volatile as investors weigh uncertainty in the Middle East, rising interest rates and concerns about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade.

Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking a stable income stream can enhance their portfolios with the addition of dividend stocks. In this regard, tracking recommendations of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks that are backed by strong financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is the first dividend pick. Earlier this month, the company paid a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share. At an annualized dividend of $7.12 per share, CVX offers a dividend yield of about 3.5%.

After a meeting with senior leadership, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on CVX and raised his price target to $240 from $225. Among the key takeaways Mehta emphasized was that Chevron is building a solid pipeline of promising international exploration projects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean. In particular, he mentioned the focus on Venezuela, where Chevron expects to more than double gross production to 600 kbd from about 280 kbd by 2031, using three joint ventures.

Mehta also noted Chevron's efforts to deploy new technologies, especially in shale and tight-oil operations. CVX is focused on boosting productivity and efficiency to maximize free cash flow, he added.

Initiatives such as artificial lift optimization, AI and machine learning, and advanced chemical treatments are expected to improve recovery by about 10% in new wells. Consequently, capital spending per barrel in U.S. shale is projected to fall 25% in 2026, with total Permian spending dropping below $3.5 billion.

Mehta also said that Chevron sees power as a growth opportunity and is using its U.S. natural gas portfolio, key equipment and partnerships to meet rising demand. Notably, Chevron recently signed a 20-year power purchase deal with Microsoft (MSFT) to supply 2.67 GW of behind-the-meter capacity. This project, called Kilby, is expected to see its first power delivery in 2028 and generate mid-teens returns and long-term cash flows.

Mehta ranks No. 536 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 11.3%. See Chevron Statistics on TipRanks.

Enterprise Products Partners (EPD) is a master limited partnership (MLP) that provides midstream energy services to producers and consumers of natural gas, natural gas liquids (NGLs), crude oil, refined products and petrochemicals. With a quarterly cash distribution of 56 cents per common unit ($2.24 per common unit annualized), EPD offers a yield of about 6%.

Following a recent call with the company, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on EPD with a price target of $42. The analyst slightly lowered her estimates for the second half of 2026, citing margin and volume normalization, though demand dynamics remain strong.

While the 5-star analyst expects a sequential decline due to normal seasonality and normalization following high spreads in the first half of the year, she still expects EPD to report another solid quarter (Q3 2026) and enter 2027 with a favorable setup. Ahead of Q3 earnings, Scotto expects investors to focus on export demand durability, EPD's ability to profit from spot cargo sales and growth in Permian volumes.

Scotto continues to expect $150 million of common unit buybacks per quarter in the second half of 2026. She expects buybacks to increase to $200 million per quarter in 2027, as capex spending declines and free cash flow improves.

In total, Scotto is bullish on EPD and views it as a core MLP holding that has both offensive and defensive characteristics. The analyst praised EPD's diverse asset base as ensuring stable cash flows. "Furthermore, the partnership's multi-year organic growth backlog helps provide visibility on long-term distribution growth," Scotto said.

Scotto ranks No. 199 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.9%. See Enterprise Products Partners Financials on TipRanks.

Brookfield Infrastructure Partners (BIP) operates a diversified portfolio of high-quality utilities, transport, midstream and data assets. The company declared a quarterly distribution of 45.5 cents per unit, payable on September 29. At an annualized distribution of $1.82, BIP offers a yield of 5.2%.

Recently, BMO Capital analyst Devin Dodge reaffirmed a buy rating on Brookfield Infrastructure with a price target of $47. The analyst cited improved visibility into BIP's growth and views its corporate simplification as a potential catalyst.

"Layering in the benefits from the proposed corporate simplification, a multiple well below historical levels and an attractive yield, we believe BIP offers a compelling risk/reward and it remains a preferred idea," said Dodge.

With more organic growth projects in the pipeline and new partnership investment opportunities, Dodge believes that BIP has clearer visibility into where it will deploy capital. That should support continued double-digit growth in funds from operations (FFO) per unit.

Dodge said the semiconductor foundries that BIP is developing with Intel (INTC) are expected to be fully commissioned by the end of 2026, with returns improving through Q4 2026. Dodge expects the joint venture to boost FFO by 300-400 basis points.

Dodge ranks No. 476 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 68% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.5%. See Brookfield Infrastructure Partners KPIs on TipRanks.

#2898. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2890. Austin Reaves remains out vs. Jazz, Walker Kessler returns

Following a dramatic overtime win against the Hornets on Tuesday, the Lakers will still be at nearly full strength against the Jazz on the second night of a back-to-back, with one major exception.

Austin Reaves will miss his third game since suffering a heel bruise against the Bucks last Wednesday. However, he is the only player on the injury report for the purple and gold with Walker Kessler, who also missed Tuesday's game, set to return.

There are multiple players notable in their exclusion from this injury report. First, as noted, Kessler will return after being out with leg soreness on Tuesday.

"Walker, some lower leg soreness, got bumped," head coach JJ Redick said before Tuesday's game. "Just thinking big picture. We don't think it's serious, but it makes sense for him to, as a precautionary, be out tonight."

Luka Dončić playing both legs of the back-to-back is notable, too. Luka's two missed games this season have both been during a back-to-back, but he did play in the team's previous back-to-back at home.

As for Austin, Redick said Tuesday that he would do on-court work Wednesday morning to see where he's at. The injury and return remain a pain tolerance matter with Redick having previously said that there is no structural damage with the injury.

It is easier to be patient with Reaves' return with the Lakers winning. But with a match-up with the Spurs looming, it would also be nice to see him return soon so he can take part in that contest.

As for the Jazz, who are also on the second night of a back-to-back, their injury report is nearly the same as it was against the Clippers. Jaxson Hayes, who was out on Tuesday, has been upgraded to questionable.

Jaren Jackson, Lauri Markkanen and Jusuf Nurkic are all out. Kobe Bufkin also will not be making his return to LA as he is with Utah's two-way team.

#2889. Federal Reserve Board releases results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families

The Federal Reserve Board on Friday released the results of the 2025 Survey of Consumer Finances, which provides the public and policymakers with detailed insights into the economic condition of American families.

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The current version of the survey has been undertaken every three years since 1989 and provides the public with an extensive time series of thorough information on the economic conditions of American families. Alongside the survey data, the Board issued its summary report entitled Changes in U.S. Family Finances from 2022 to 2025 (PDF), which examines changes in income, net worth, assets, debt, and financial vulnerability.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2885. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."
